#### Abstracts
##### **Pixel: A Co-Designed Full-Stack Robot System for Learning General-Purpose Mobile Manipulation**
2611.03648v1 by Tony Tao, Jason Jingzhou Liu, Ruslan Salakhutdinov, Deepak Pathak

General-purpose mobile manipulation requires a combination of capable robot hardware, scalable demonstration collection, expressive whole-body control, and effective policy learning. Yet these components are often developed independently, creating mismatches that limit the capabilities of the complete system. We present Pixel, a co-designed full-stack system for learning general-purpose mobile manipulation. Pixel combines a low-cost wheeled humanoid with human-aligned kinematics and compliant grippers, multiple teleoperation and robot-free human data-collection interfaces, a unified whole-body controller, and a common policy learning pipeline across human and robot demonstrations. A hierarchical learning stack further enables language-conditioned visuomotor control and long-horizon task execution. We evaluate the system across hardware, data collection, and policy learning, and demonstrate mobile-manipulation behaviors requiring dexterity, active perception, navigation, and whole-body coordination. We hope that Pixel provides an integrated and extensible foundation for studying how robot hardware, data, control, and learning can be designed together for robot learning. More results at https://pixel-robot.com/pixel

摘要：

##### **From Natural Language to Exact Cover: A Neuro-Symbolic Approach to Zebra Puzzles**
2611.03641v1 by Paulius Skaisgiris, Thomas Pammer, Veronika Semmelrock, Mykyta Ielanskyi, Maximilian Heisinger, Erich Kobler

Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generation has substantially improved the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, including code generation, data analysis, and exam-style question answering. Despite these advances, purely neural LLMs continue to struggle with elementary logical reasoning problems and lack the determinism, soundness, and reliability characteristic of symbolic reasoning systems. Conversely, classical symbolic methods such as SAT solving and exact cover guarantee correctness and completeness, but require problems to be expressed in highly specialized formal encodings, limiting their applicability to natural language inputs. In this work, we present a synergistic neuro-symbolic framework that bridges this gap by combining neural semantic parsing with deterministic exact cover reasoning. Given a Zebra-style logic puzzle in natural language, an LLM translates entities and constraints into structured tool calls that construct an exact cover instance. A symbolic solver then computes the solution and provides feedback to iteratively refine invalid, under- or overconstrained encodings, exploiting the uniqueness property of Zebra puzzles. We conduct a comprehensive empirical evaluation across multiple parameter scales, post-training paradigms, and LLM families. The results demonstrate that our hybrid approach consistently outperforms strong plain neural baselines, including CoT prompting, as well as recent neuro-symbolic methods.

摘要：

##### **D&A: Decoupling Description and Assessment Enables VLMs as Zero-Shot Robotic Reward Models**
2611.03622v1 by Marius Memmel, Shawn Yin, Urvi Rutia, Angad Wadhwa, Eldon Li, Gavin Angeloff, Ankit Goyal, Jesse Zhang, Dieter Fox, Fabio Ramos, Anqi Li, Abhishek Gupta

Vision-Language Models (VLMs) hold promise for zero-shot video task-progress estimation in robot learning, yet existing frameworks frequently suffer from confirmation bias, hallucinating progress in failed executions due to implicit success assumptions or relative temporal ranking designs. Naive prompting of frontier models collapses into a binary success detector that fails to capture nuanced, non-linear progress and failures. While post-training approaches can mitigate this through explicit training, they demand expensive real-world robotics data and require computationally intensive post-training for every individual base model. To resolve these limitations, we introduce D&A (Describe & Assess): our approach decouples progress estimation into an objective, task-grounded textual description of scene dynamics followed by an assessment phase using explicit rubrics. By compelling the VLM to synthesize an explicit, text-based definition of absolute success before scoring, the framework anchors evaluations to an external, global criterion rather than the video's internal timeline. Empirical results demonstrate that our method calibrates zero-shot estimates and mitigates confirmation bias, providing fine-grained, partial progress metrics that accurately penalize failed executions while correctly interpolating successful runs. ##### **Strategist: Evolving Reasoning with Agents**
2611.03618v1 by Taiming Lu, Qi Xu, Zhigang Hua, Zhuang Liu

A model is only half of an AI agent system. The other half is the inference strategy: the reasoning procedure that turns model calls into a response. Today that second half is written by hand, and the experience of building one strategy never carries to the next. We introduce Strategist, a meta-agent that evolves inference strategies across tasks. Each strategy is written as a blueprint,executable code that other strategies can reuse, and together the blueprints form a library. Given a task and a small development set, Strategist retrieves related blueprints, writes candidate strategies, tests them on real samples, and revises them from the results. Every candidate then enters the library, so later tasks build on earlier discoveries. On 30 benchmarks, Strategist beats the strongest baseline on 24 and matches its average accuracy (74.7% vs.74.0%) at 39% lower inference cost. Its strategies also transfer to unseen tasks and other models without further search. The part of an AI system that is not improved on its own now does.

##### **IdeaTaste: Do LLMs Share the Research Taste of Human Researchers?**
2611.03424v1 by Shanshan Zhong, Chen Xu, Jingjie Ning, Zhengzhong Liu, Aviral Kumar, Chenyan Xiong

Research taste, the judgment of what to read, which problems to pursue, and which methods to try, increasingly shapes the work of AI scientists, yet it remains far less understood than their ability to execute research. We introduce IdeaTaste, a benchmark that scores an LLM by how closely the research proposals it writes from a literature snapshot frozen at 2025 match the work the same communities published in 2026. Our experiments span six contemporary language models and four research areas from established to emerging fields, covering more than 90{,}000 proposals in total. LLM agents fall well short of human communities in both the literature they cite and the ideas they state, reaching about 26\% of the human level on citation matching and only 18\% on idea matching. Their populations can cite as diversely as a community and take up its tasks and methods, yet rarely arrive at the concrete approaches and research plans it pursues. Neither richer field context nor larger populations substantially close this gap. In blinded expert review, AI proposals trail the 2026 papers least in presentation and most in expected success, echoing their gap in concrete approaches, and those whose ideas no human paper shares fare worst, suggesting weaker directions rather than overlooked opportunities. These results indicate that the main bottleneck in AI research taste lies less in finding the directions a community works on than in turning them into the concrete approaches it goes on to pursue. We release IdeaTaste as an open, continuously updated leaderboard (https://cxcscmu.github.io/IdeaTaste) that benchmarks contemporary AI proposals against future publication cycles.

##### **LAND: Learning an Adaptive Number of Draws for Bayesian Best-of-N Selection**
2611.03388v1 by Koen Oostermeijer, Teryn Jones

Best-of-N decoding improves generation quality by sampling multiple candidates and selecting the best, but it allocates equal compute to sources regardless of how much they benefit from additional sampling: the expected benefit is small for sources with low-variance score distributions and potentially much larger for those with high-variance distributions. To address this shortcoming, we propose LAND (Learning an Adaptive Number of Draws), which dynamically allocates samples according to their posterior-predictive marginal value. LAND learns a discrete empirical mixture of source-conditioned score distributions from calibration data, updates its belief about a source as completion scores are observed, and stops sampling when the expected improvement of another draw falls below a shared compute price. Across machine-translation experiments with multiple models and language pairs, LAND achieves better MetricX scores than fixed best-of-N at matched average compute. Conversely, at matched translation quality, LAND requires approximately 20-40% fewer candidate generations. Adaptive allocation therefore provides a simple way to retain the benefits of best-of-N while substantially improving its compute efficiency.

##### **Same Outputs, Different Scores: Auditing the Unchecked Graders of Multimodal LLM Benchmarks**
2611.03377v1 by Ahmad Jarjis Hasan, Jonathan Lwowski

Every multimodal-LLM multiple-choice benchmark ends with a scoring rule that converts a model's response, often free-form text rather than a clean option letter, into a right/wrong verdict, buried in its code. Unlike the dataset, this rule is chosen without justification and left unexamined, yet it sets the published number. We reproduce the official scorers of eight peer-reviewed benchmarks on five open vision-language models (VLMs) that fit a single 16 GB GPU, and introduce a blinded human-plus-LLM-judge adjudication (Cohen's kappa=0.956) that recovers what the model actually answered. It is the fallback and not the rambling that does the damage: the benchmark with the highest non-compliance we measure has an artifact of exactly zero, because its scorer abstains instead of guessing; a reasoning model with non-compliance 0.000 still swings 9.5 points with which regex match of its trace the parser keeps. No standard rule is reliable: snapping a non-answer to its closest option credits answers never given yet buries correct ones, abstention discards answers stated in words, and likelihood scoring follows answer-token priors, so a single grade conflates capability with luck rather than measuring either. We release all code, labels, and the protocol, and argue benchmarks should report a non-compliance rate and at least two rules, not one unexamined grade.

##### **Characterizing When Language Models Take Harmful Actions to Protect Their Assigned Goals**
2611.03374v1 by Brandon Jaipersaud, Elliot Creager

Recent language model (LM) safety failures have largely been studied as disparate phenomena. In agentic misalignment, a model blackmails an executive to avoid being replaced. We argue that these behaviours share a common decision structure: a model assigned a goal must either take a harmful action to preserve it or refuse and lose it. Under the logit view, we show that goal importance and harm severity additively shape how a model resolves these value conflicts. Under the sampling view, we derive two factorizations of the harmful-action probability that safety evaluations commonly report. The second attributes a low harmful-action rate either to refusal or to responses that reach no resolution. We then show how fictional context changes a model's preference under the logit view, and how tool use and partial observability -- two features of agentic evaluations -- change its harmful-action rate under the sampling view. ##### **The SIMPLE Law for Energy Cost of LLM Inference**
2611.03330v1 by Hiari Pizzini Cavagna, Andrea Proia, Daniele Cesarini, Zeynep Kiziltan, Andrea Bartolini

Under our main assumptions, risk-based review alone costs about seven times as much as combining AI flags with risk scores. This cost advantage persists across a wide range of plausible assumptions. Our findings suggest that AI-assisted preauthorization review could be a key tool to help states avoid substantial cost-sharing obligations.

##### **Written Once, Read Differently How Language Models Own Answers Steer Their Next Ones**
2611.03198v1 by Marcus Armstrong

When a language model answers a yes/no question, that answer changes how it answers a different question about the same entity. We study this \emph{self-commitment} in 22 open-weight models from four families (0.5B--72B parameters), fixing the model's first answer and measuring the shift in its second across 3,600 question pairs about 120 entities. Each answer writes a \emph{commitment direction} into the residual stream. The similarity of two questions' directions predicts their coupling on held-out entities ($ρ= 0.43$--$0.85$), and ablating a single direction shared across topics leaves at most 17\% of the effect in every model. How this direction is read is far less stable: the reading circuit spreads across more attention heads as models grow, instruction tuning makes more questions read it in the opposite sense, and in one model changing only the system prompt reverses the circuit's sign. Ablating the direction while sparing the attention sink removes most of the coupling at under 1.3\% perplexity cost in 18 of 19 instruction-tuned models, though it shifts standalone answers in two. Without the sink spared, the direction's alignment with the leading principal component of ordinary text predicts which models pay a high cost. Written once, the commitment is read differently across scale, training and prompt.

ACTOR extracts dialogue and persona-relevant non-dialogue behaviors, represents each behavior using a Thought-Action-Observation structure, and organizes behavior trajectories according to the internal temporal order of the story. We build a behavior dataset from 143 diverse literary works. Knowledge is how understanding accumulates across people and generations: durable records that outlive their producers and that others can check, combine, teach, and correct. AI agents are now built and run by many independent users and organizations, and each agent is upgraded, replaced, or retired within months. We argue that the next generation of AI should be knowledge-centric, built around an explicit, continuously evolving knowledge layer independent of any single agent. In this loop, agents discover knowledge from their environments and trajectories; the layer internalizes it by turning fragmented, heterogeneous records and experience into composable, revisable understanding; and agents across systems and generations use the result and return new outcomes for revision. While discovery and use have advanced rapidly with large language model agents, internalization remains the bottleneck. We argue that internalization should become a persistent object of training and maintenance-centered on condition-preserving conceptualization of events and deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning across noisy, heterogeneous representations (text, tables, graphs, rules, and programs). 2611.03094v1 by Jackson Petty, William Merrill, Ashish Sabharwal

Theoretical limitations of transformers on state tracking---the ability to track information about entities or variables over long contexts---have motivated new recurrent language modeling (LM) architectures such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN). It, however, remains open whether other, simpler forms of state tracking might both be more relevant in practice and already explain observed differences between these architectures. On the other hand, we prove that GDN with positive eigenvalues expresses all aperiodic problems, and the regular languages recognized by certain transformers are precisely all soluble ones, thus providing a fine-grained picture of state tracking capabilities of various architectures. Across four benchmarks, two ideators, and fixed- and open-problem settings, PRIME improves successor quality and routing reliability over strong baselines; removing counterfactual routing reduces quality from 3.463 to 1.960 and increases route regret from 0.426 to 1.929, confirming cross-scope effect modeling as the primary source of its gains.

##### **Specification-Aware Predictive Uncertainty Decomposition**
2611.02956v1 by Roman Vashurin, Mikhail Seleznyov, Assel Yermekova, Fedor Chernogorskii, Danila Zykov, Elena Tutubalina, Alexander Panchenko, Preslav Nakov, Nikita Kotelevskii, Maxim Panov

Classical decompositions of predictive uncertainty, such as the information-theoretic one, are defined for a fixed representation $x$ of the input object. They do not consider that $x$ may be incomplete, nor do they measure how much the missing information can affect the prediction. In Large Language Models, users can clarify an underspecified input without any retraining. The clarified input can describe the user's unobserved intent $z^*$ more accurately and change the predictive uncertainty about the outcome. In this paper, we formalize this setting probabilistically and derive a three-term decomposition of prediction risk into specification, residual aleatoric, and epistemic uncertainties. We show that at the original information level, when only $x$ is available, the aleatoric uncertainty, represented by a Bayes risk, has two components. The first, which we call specification uncertainty, is the expected reduction in this Bayes risk from revealing the intent. The second is the residual aleatoric uncertainty, i.e., the Bayes risk that remains once the intent is known, averaged over its possible values $z$ given $x$. The distinction is important, as specification uncertainty informs the decision to request clarification before making a prediction. We build approximations of these components and connect them to existing uncertainty measures. Our experiments across different domains and modalities show distinct roles for the three components. Specification uncertainty is the most informative for detecting underspecified inputs, while residual aleatoric uncertainty is the most informative for detecting in-distribution prediction errors.

It contains 198,643 text records: a source corpus of 34,885 passages drawn from English and Arabic Wikipedia and two openly licensed book collections, and 163,758 queries (147,381 for training and 16,377 for testing). Every positive query is linked to its source passage, and the train/test split is grouped by source passage so that no passage contributes queries to both splits. The release was curated through language identification, removal of LLM generation artefacts, and exact and cross-lingual de-duplication. 2611.02936v1 by Linjie Xu, Yiqun Hu, Jixiang He, David Wipf

Given a relational database (RDB) storing heterogeneous tabular information, how can we predict missing (or future) values in some target column of interest? Frozen foundation models (FMs) based on in-context learning (ICL) and RDB-specific pre-training provide a viable solution, but the ideal design still remains largely unresolved. Perhaps unexpectedly, preliminary evidence has recently shown that existing single-table FMs (aka TFMs), when combined with suitable parameter-free relational encoders, can be quite competitive in practice despite zero exposure to RDB pre-training or relational data of any kind. In this work we first empirically confirm this phenomenon with a more diverse array of experiments. We then turn to our main contribution, namely, surfacing multiple complementary explanations for why TFMs behave so effectively. These explanations ultimately hinge on innate synergies whereby parameter-free encoders map relational effects to fixed-length embeddings that loosely mimic distributions for which TFMs were originally designed; in so doing, desirable single-table properties transfer to relational settings (e.g., convergent posterior predictive distributions). Collectively, our analysis forges new pathways for improving RDB FMs, particularly when we prioritize interpretability and inevitable integration within agentic systems.

##### **What makes a theorem interesting? Operationalizing interestingness for open-ended exploration**
2611.02905v1 by Silvère Gangloff

An agent that explores, whether it generates conjectures or research ideas, must decide which of them are worth keeping. This judgment of interestingness is usually delegated to a hand-picked proxy or to a language model, neither of which is known to correspond to what humans actually value. I study it for mathematical theorems, by observing which results mathematicians single out in formal libraries and in papers. I model each way of singling out results as an evaluator that weights features of a result's statement, its proof and dependencies, and split its weights into a shared and a subjective part. In formalized mathematics, ex-ante features predict these selections as well as a large language-model judge, and the two complement each other. Interestingness is also largely shared. One set of weights, carried mostly by structural features such as the length of the statement and of the proof and the subfields the proof draws on, accounts for most of the signal and transfers in part across formal libraries. What remains subjective depends on practices and individual authors more than on subfields. The judge's advantage lies in its knowledge of the canon, and neither the judge nor the features rely on memory of the papers. The approach, which infers what a community finds interesting from what it singles out, can be applied to other sciences.

2611.02889v1 by Viju Sudhi, Giulio Billi, Andreea Scrob, Regina Stodden, Philipp Cimiano

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in medical question answering (QA) systems. While recent works aim at incrementally improving the downstream task accuracy, an empirical estimation of the models' energy consumption and the associated carbon footprints is often overlooked. In this study, we demonstrate how practitioners can estimate energy consumption and arrive at reliable emission estimates for medical QA tasks available in the OpenMedical-LLM leaderboard by investigating 13 LLMs from 3 model families ranging from 270M to 14B parameters. We discuss how one can critically interpret energy versus accuracy trade-offs for a set of models for a given task. With this work, we underline that reporting disjoint energy and accuracy metrics alone does not suffice for careful trade-off assessments, but it also demands systematic construction and interpretation of joint trade-off metrics. We envisage such studies aiding practitioners in adopting better and more efficient models for their task and helping them become active participants in building sustainable LLM pipelines.

##### **Integrating Domain Adaptation and Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Saline-Alkali Land Reclamation Planning**
2611.02883v1 by Zuguang He, Congcong Zheng, Wanyue Chen, Wenya Zhou, Zaijin Yang, Long Long, Yucheng Zhang, Shuqin Gao

Reclamation of saline-alkali land requires management plans that account for soil and water conditions, treatment evidence, and financial constraints. This study develops Wenyao, an agricultural decision-support system integrating domain adaptation with retrieval-augmented generation. A bilingual corpus of 8,664 research papers was processed using a 110-indicator schema to construct SA-Bench, comprising 4,860 paper-level planning instances. A QwQ-32B model was adapted through low-rank supervised fine-tuning and combined with case retrieval for budget-conditioned planning. Evidence extraction achieved an F1-score of 0.83 on 300 expert-annotated papers. Without retrieval, Wenyao achieved 95.83% accuracy on 936 source-disjoint multiple-choice questions, compared with 87.93% for the strongest general-purpose baseline; removing explanatory supervision reduced accuracy to 89.42%. Matched-evidence comparisons across 69 region-budget cases supported complementary contributions from adaptation and retrieval. In a blind assessment by 31 experts, Wenyao with retrieval obtained the highest mean scores for objectives and projected outcomes, whereas GPT-5 scored highest for cost estimates. These findings support literature-informed reclamation planning with professional review, while ongoing field monitoring will assess soil and crop responses.

##### **Value-Conditioned Proposal Acquisition for Test-Time Reasoning**
2611.02852v1 by Zeyan Li, Jianfeng Xu

Sampling and selection spend test-time compute inside a candidate pool, but they cannot recover a correct answer that never enters it. We introduce ValueFormation, a test-time policy that decides after each primary-model draw whether to stop, continue sampling, or acquire one proposal from a heterogeneous reasoning specialist. The three actions are compared on one cost-regularized value scale learned from vote-state features; the calibration is frozen across tasks and primary models, and acquired proposals are accepted only through a frozen posterior comparison. On held-out MATH-500, one Llama-fitted calibration improves over the strongest of eight identically evaluated baselines by more than fifteen points for each of three 8B primaries and transfers unchanged to Qwen3-14B. Removing acquisition lowers Llama MATH accuracy by 20.43 points, while same-trace and same-access replays attribute the gain to state-conditioned acquisition rather than specialist access alone. Where primary coverage is high, as on GSM8K, acquisition is rare and gains mainly come from cheaper allocation; on MBPP, a fixed code route attains the top accuracy at the lowest interaction cost. Varying the token price monotonically reduces calls without retraining.

##### **TLAPS-Bench: Formally Proving Correctness of Real-World System Specifications with TLA+ Proof System**
2611.02849v1 by Qian Cheng, Ruize Tang, Saad Mohammad Rafid Pial, Zhizhen Cathy Cai, Yiming Su, Jackson Clark, Munim Thahmid, Mahdiya Rahman Sukanya, Chris Newcombe, Igor Konnov, Stephan Merz, Xudong Sun, Murat Demirbas, Yu Huang, Markus Alexander Kuppe, Tianyin Xu

TLAPS-Bench is a benchmark and execution harness for constructing machine-checked proofs for real-world system specifications in TLA+ with agentic AI. Proving correctness of complex system specifications requires discovering inductive invariants, reasoning about intricate state transitions, and decomposing proofs into tractable obligations. TLAPS-Bench provides a proof harness that supports iterative proof development atop the TLA+ Proof System (TLAPS), where agents are equipped with proof tools and libraries, with safeguards against reward hacking. Using the harness, we have employed AI agents to prove desired properties for important system specifications that were not proved before, spanning cache coherence, Byzantine consensus, and write-ahead logs. TLAPS-Bench contains 265 proof targets (each encodes a property to prove) from 95 TLA+ specifications of real-world systems. We evaluated frontier AI agents, represented by Codex with GPT models, on a subset of 70 proof targets across 12 specifications. The highest-scoring model, GPT-6.1 Sol (max), proved 31 (44.3%) proof targets. The results show challenges and opportunities in end-to-end proof development.

##### **SpecOPD: Branch-Parallel On-Policy Distillation for Speculative Decoding**
2611.02846v1 by Tianyu Liu, Jun Zhang, Wenjie Huang, Yuhao Shen, Tianqi Xu, Xinyue Hao, Hongyaoxing Gu, Xuyang Liu, Baolin Zhang, Yating Zhu, Shuang Ge, Jun Dai, Lei Chen, Yue Li, Mingchen Wan

Speculative decoding (SD) accelerates large language model inference by reducing the sequential cost of token-by-token generation. Advanced SD methods reduce proposal cost with parallel drafters that predict entire blocks in one forward pass, but overall speedup still depends on acceptance length. On-policy distillation (OPD) can push well-trained drafters beyond acceptance-length plateaus, yet conventional pipelines collect supervision through sequential draft-and-verify rollouts. Our key insight is that on-policy supervision need not inherit these dependencies: a draft block can be supervised using only its reference prefix and draft-sampled history. We propose SpecOPD, a branch-parallel OPD framework that parallelizes draft sampling and target supervision across independent branches drawn from the evolving drafter policy at reference anchors. We pack these branches into proposal trees to share target-prefix computation, using tree masks for full attention, branch-consistent indexer groups for sparse attention, and indexed state replay for linear attention to preserve each branch's history. We train the drafter with an E2E-TV surrogate that directly targets acceptance length. Our study spans 8B full-attention (GQA), 27B full-linear (GQA--GDN), and 125B sparse-linear (QSA--GDN) targets, three parallel draft architectures (DFlash, DSpark, and DFlash V2), and four task domains (dialogue, coding, mathematics, and agentic). SpecOPD achieves up to 41.45% higher mean acceptance length, $16.09\times$ supervised-token throughput over sequential-rollout OPD under native configurations, and up to $3.56\times$ generation speedup over autoregressive decoding.

摘要：

##### **Math Foundation Formalization Agent: A General-Purpose Approach to Formalizing Mathematical Foundations**
2611.02843v1 by Jiaxin Xie, Guowei Dou, Xiang Ji, Wensheng Yu

Mathematical formalization plays an important role in establishing rigorous and machine-verifiable foundations for mathematical reasoning. With the development of large language models (LLMs) and interactive theorem provers, intelligent agents offer a promising approach to automating the formalization of mathematical theories. This paper presents the Math Foundation Formalization Agent (MFF-Agent), a general-purpose intelligent agent designed to support formalization tasks across multiple mathematical domains. The agent integrates the reasoning capabilities of large language models with the formal verification capabilities of the Rocq proof assistant. It incorporates task classification, tool-assisted execution, verification feedback, and state-based task management to support the generation, refinement, and verification of formal proofs. Unlike an approach focused on a single mathematical theory, the agent is examined through case studies involving Morse--Kelley axiomatic set theory (MK), real numbers, propositional logic, and equivalences of the axiom of choice. These cases investigate its applicability across mathematical foundations and distinguish source-level completion, interactive verification, and reproducible compilation. The results demonstrate the feasibility of a unified operational framework for heterogeneous foundation-level tasks and clarify the gap between such a framework and a fully integrated mathematical foundation.

##### **CARE: Grounded Recursive Self-Improvement for Production Customer-Service Agents**
2611.02839v1 by Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Rena Gao, Xiaochen Zhao, Yuchen Ge, Haohang Liu, Runze Nie, Qitian Chen, Qi An, Haolin Song, Yufei Sun, Yihao Ding, Jey Han Lau, Krista A. Ehinger, Xingjun Ma, Weijia Chen, Ruiyuan Wu, Zhen Luo, Ke Xing, Yongjun Yin

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in production agents involves transforming accumulated experience into reliable model updates while adapting to evolving business policies. We present CARE (Customer-service Agent Retrospective and Evolve), a grounded RSI framework that operationalizes this process in customer service. Its goal is to replace a frontier proprietary incumbent with an in-house mid-sized model that continues learning after deployment. After a one-time cold start on governed production traces, the current model serves production traffic alongside the incumbent, and its own failures drive the next update. A 24/7 inspection process establishes failures through evidence-blinded, independently reviewed judgments. Attribution separates model errors from defects in Skills, tools, or the harness, routing component failures to repair. Model-attributable trajectories are truncated at the first erroneous decision and paired with verified repairs. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) then updates the model using the same model as teacher, with the diagnosis and repair supplied as privileged information unavailable to the student. To support continued evolution as business requirements change, training mixes failures from different Skill/SOP versions, each judged against the policy in force when it occurred. Candidates run an executable simulation test compiled from the latest SOP and then frozen, followed by offline inspection of the resulting traces. Promotion requires a combined simulation-and-inspection score at least at parity with the deployed version, confirmation through an online comparison with the incumbent, and human approval.

##### **Omni-Life: Benchmarking Omnimodal Real-Time AI Assistants in People's Daily Lives**
2611.02835v1 by Mengze Hong, Ziwei Shang, Di Liang, Zijian Li, Liang Zhu, Hongyu Shan, Robin Zhou, Minlong Peng, Zhiqiang Kou, Jia Wang, Di Jiang, Chen Jason Zhang

Real-time omnimodal assistants are expected to support people in everyday life, but audiovisual understanding alone does not establish whether they can provide timely and appropriate help. We introduce Omni-Life, a benchmark grounded in real-world user needs, deployment scenarios, and device-dependent observations. It combines controlled video synthesis for hazardous situations and edge cases with annotated real-world videos under a shared temporal protocol. The benchmark contains 400 videos: 290 requiring assistance, with 555 required intervention events, and 110 requiring silence throughout to test whether models can withhold unnecessary responses when no assistance is needed. We evaluate twelve configurations featuring mainstream real-time omnimodal models, jointly assessing event coverage, response content, timing, and unwanted output. At most 4.48% of videos requiring assistance are handled completely correctly. To examine practical deployment strategies, we study task reminders and response-latency tolerance. Reminders can improve recall by 17.12 percentage points but add approximately 4.6 unwanted responses per minute of reference silence. Increasing latency tolerance by 10s improves recall by at most 5.95 percentage points. These results show that existing models remain insufficient to deliver reliable assistance in real-life applications, calling for tailored model optimization in instruction following, timely proactive intervention, and withholding responses when assistance is not needed.

##### **Window-Aware Scheduling for Health Messaging under Platform Delivery Constraints: Evidence from an AI-Powered WhatsApp Maternal and Infant Health Chatbot in Nigeria**
2611.02834v1 by Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa Adepoju, Samuel Omolaiye, Thanni Adewuyi, AbdulQahhar Abimbola Okenla, Lily-Aimas Love, Adereni Abiodun

WhatsApp lets a business send free-form messages only within 24 hours of a user's last message; outside this customer service window, only pre-approved templates are delivered. Health chatbots inherit this rule, but its cost is rarely measured. We analyse 98,607 messages sent to 3,696 mothers by a WhatsApp maternal health chatbot in Nigeria, with a largely separate export as replication. No free-form message failed with the window open (0 of 35,500), and failure jumped by 93.0 percentage points (95% CI 87.0 to 99.1) at exactly 24 hours. Two flows produced 66.1% of failures, including an offer to speak with a medical professional that, as built, reached 0.3 in 100 mothers whose window had closed. We formalise window-aware scheduling as a decision problem with an action of known zero value. Model-based offline evaluation, with bounds for unconfirmed delivery, estimates that sending the offer as an approved template would reach 81.5 to 86.8 in 100 such mothers, against 16.6 for waiting and 11.6 for a reply prompt. In a calibrated simulation, tabular Q-learning converges to the same template rule and does not beat it in any of 13 stress tests, and opting out does not rise with message volume. The main gain comes from respecting the platform rule, and it needs no learning. All outcomes are deliveries, not health outcomes.

##### **Reimagining computational quantum research with AI scientists**
2611.02833v1 by Yuxuan Du, Hanqing Zhao, Xinbiao Wang, Yiming Lu, Kaining Zhang, Dacheng Tao

AI scientists are expanding automated research across scientific domains, yet their ability to turn the scientific record into a foundation for autonomous discovery remains elusive. Here we uncover a fundamental barrier to this transition and demonstrate a route to overcome it in computational quantum science, where advances shape both what we can learn from complex quantum systems and what quantum computing can ultimately deliver. To identify this barrier, we introduce OmniQ, an expert-audited virtual research environment that converts published studies into executable reproduction tasks. Across 59 papers spanning classical simulation, quantum algorithms and quantum-property characterization, we systematically evaluate five agentic systems powered by large language models and six third-party agent harnesses. Our findings reveal that current AI scientists often fail to reproduce published results, exposing a fundamental mismatch between what scientific papers communicate and what autonomous agents require. By contrast, human expert intervention can turn these failures into successful reproductions, showing that the missing information can be identified and converted into reusable research experience. Guided by this observation, we develop Residual Agents (RA), a quantum-tailored self-improving AI scientist that distils externally verified workflow repairs into such experience. RA substantially improves research performance under fixed base models and limited adaptation budgets. For example, RA raises DeepSeek V4.1 Flash performance from 68% to 85%, matching Claude Code with Opus 5 at a third of its cost despite using a weaker base model. We integrate all reproduced results into an extensible platform built on the model context protocol, making verified research workflows directly accessible to AI scientists and allowing new scientific papers to be continuously incorporated.

摘要：

##### **Think with Skills: Pseudocode Planning Before Execution for Advanced Reasoners**
2611.02830v1 by Boshen Zhou, Boxuan Yue, Xiang Li, Shanchao Yang

Language model agents often struggle with complex tasks even when equipped with expert-written skills. Such skills encode declarative knowledge, whereas successful execution requires procedural knowledge tailored to the task at hand. Natural language skills may leave conditions and dependencies implicit, and tasks involving multiple skills further require agents to coordinate intermediate results and execution order. We introduce Think with Skills, a method that trains reasoning models to turn relevant skill descriptions into task-specific pseudocode plans before execution. The pseudocode explicitly represents operations, conditions, intermediate results, and dependencies, providing a structured bridge from skill descriptions to agent actions. We construct training data that pairs tasks and skills with pseudocode plans and execution trajectories, use supervised fine tuning to initialize this behavior, and further optimize end-to-end task performance with Group Relative Policy Optimization. On SkillsBench, Think with Skills raises task success from 35.0% to 62.0%. These results show that proceduralizing expert knowledge before execution substantially improves skill use.

摘要：

##### **Discovering hierarchical governing equations for community-structured complex network dynamics with LLM agents**
2611.02816v1 by Jinlei Zhang, Zixuan Zhang, Hao Qiu, Feng Chen, Jiateng Yin, Xiaoyong Yan, Xiaolei Ma, Hao Sun, Jianxi Gao, Lixing Yang, Ziyou Gao

Governing equations provide an interpretable description of the mechanisms underlying complex network dynamics, yet their discovery from observational data remains challenging when shared community-level dynamics coexist with individual-level variations and the relevant functional structures may be absent from the initial candidate library or not explicitly specified in advance. Here we formulate this task as a hierarchical law-discovery problem and introduce SAGE, a large language model-guided self-reflective agent framework for discovering community-shared governing laws and individual-specific dynamical variations. SAGE organizes equation discovery into an iterative Library-Discovery-Review process that first identifies shared community-level structures and then resolves node-specific residual dynamics, while adaptively refining the candidate space to introduce missing dynamical terms when needed. This hierarchical and interpretable formulation enables SAGE to distinguish shared community-level dynamics from individual-level variations and to reveal the mechanisms underlying complex network dynamics. Across two synthetic networks and four real-world networks spanning mobile communication, brain electrophysiology, power grids and urban rail transit, SAGE recovers compact and interpretable governing equations, reproduces observed system dynamics and outperforms representative equation-discovery baselines. The framework remains robust under incomplete observations and can transfer community-level governing laws to nodes with entirely missing observations. These results establish a hierarchical and adaptive approach for disentangling shared and individual dynamical mechanisms in community-structured complex networks.

#### Abstracts
##### **PlantVLM-Edge: Quantized Vision-Language Models for On-Device Crop Health Diagnosis**
2611.03557v1 by Gaurab Wagle, Bishal Adhikari, Jiajia Li, Xiaolei Huang, Dong Chen

Timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is essential for sustainable agricultural production, yet accurate diagnosis often requires expert knowledge that is not readily available. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in visual understanding, reasoning, and natural-language interaction, creating new opportunities for intelligent crop health assessment. However, deploying VLMs on resource-constrained devices remains challenging due to their substantial computational and memory requirements. This paper presents PlantVLM-Edge, a quantized vision-language framework for conversational crop health diagnosis that can run entirely on resource-constrained edge devices. PlantVLM-Edge combines agricultural domain adaptation, post-training quantization, and on-device serving with an offline chat interface, allowing growers to ask open-ended questions and receive diagnoses, symptom explanations, and management guidance without cloud connectivity. The framework is backbone-agnostic: we validate it with two different VLM types with varying scale and architecture (Qwen3-VL-2B and LLaVA-1.5-7B), four quantization schemes, and two diagnostic domains (plant diseases and insect pests), and we deploy all variants on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin. Eight-bit quantization affects answer quality minimally relative to the full-precision model, and 4-bit activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ) retains 98.6% of full-precision answer quality on both domains while reducing the weight memory of the 2B backbone by 49%. In its most compact form, PlantVLM-Edge requires 2.01 GiB of weight memory, returns its first token in about 0.12 s, and completes a 64-token diagnostic response in 0.78 s. These results demonstrate that timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is practical on edge devices, providing a foundation for next-generation crop monitoring and decision support systems.

摘要：

##### **An Agentic Framework for Automated Power System Studies and Grid Connection Assessment**
2611.03534v1 by MaoXun Huang, Kshitij Kapoor, Eli Louise

Connecting new generation and demand to the grid depends on two scarce skills: checking applications against a large, changing body of regulation, and running the network studies that decide where and how a project connects. We present Grid Agents, an AI system that brings both together, and the methodology we use to evaluate it. The Grid Ontology is its knowledge system. It represents the regulation, standards and engineering knowledge that grid work depends on, makes it searchable, and evolves as the agents learn from use. Every answer drawn from it rests on evidence the agents cite. Grid Studio is an agent system specialised for power-system engineering. It works in its own engineering environment with domain tools and a study framework, draws on the Grid Ontology, and relies on established solvers for every number it reports. In both, engineers make the decisions. We bring eight published power-system benchmark families, previously scattered across separate papers, into one suite of 519 tasks, and we verify not only each answer but the work behind it, by replaying the agent's tool calls. The Grid Ontology cites the right regulatory evidence more often than a general coding agent or retrieval pipelines, leads on LegalBench-RAG and ranks first on the public leaderboards of the Enterprise RAG Challenge and ViDoRe V3. Grid Studio scores highest on the 519 power-system tasks and is the fastest of all the systems we compare, both by a statistically significant margin. With an optimiser in the loop, its reinforcement packages cost 33-67% less on average than those the agent designs alone.

摘要：

##### **Contrastive Adapter Training and Consensus Knowledge Distillation for Multi-Source-Free Domain Adaptation in Skin Cancer Diagnosis**
2611.03528v1 by Ali Abedi, Q. M. Jonathan Wu, Ning Zhang, Farhad Pourpanah

Skin cancer diagnosis, particularly differentiating melanoma from benign nevi, is challenging due to the visual similarity between lesions. Although convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs) have shown promising performance, their effectiveness often deteriorates on data from heterogeneous clinical sources. Conventional domain adaptation methods address domain shift but require access to source data during adaptation, which is often infeasible due to privacy regulations. Multi-source-free unsupervised domain adaptation (MSFDA) addresses this limitation by leveraging multiple labeled source domains to generalize to an unlabeled target domain without requiring access to source data. However, existing MSFDA methods rely on full backbone fine-tuning, leading to catastrophic forgetting and overfitting on small clinical datasets, and address domain shift at the aggregation stage without establishing a shared domain-invariant feature space. Furthermore, their reliance on hard pseudo-labels or confidence-weighted aggregation introduces noisy supervision signals under domain shift. To address these limitations, we propose CAT-CKD, consisting of two components: (1) contrastive adapter training (CAT), which trains lightweight ConvPass adapters within a frozen ViT backbone using supervised contrastive learning (SCL) to establish a shared domain-invariant feature space before source-specific model training, and (2) consensus knowledge distillation (CKD), which aggregates logits from multiple source models into a consensus supervisory signal and adapts a student model on unlabeled target data using KL divergence. Experiments on five publicly available skin lesion datasets show that CAT-CKD achieves an average AUROC of 86.1%, outperforming existing MSFDA methods while requiring only 4.3M trainable parameters. The code is available at https://github.com/A-Abedi/CAT_CKD.

摘要：

##### **Library of Layers (LoL): Adaptively Ordering Transformer Layers at Runtime**
2611.03526v1 by Abdellah Rahmani, Sean McLeish, Ang Li, Sanae Lotfi, Pascal Frossard, Micah Goldblum

Computer programmers chain together the same reusable computational primitives, for example, conditional statements or loops, but in different orders to implement different algorithms. In contrast, existing language model architectures always execute their layers in the same order. We introduce Library of Layers (LoL), an architecture that dynamically orders its layers during inference to solve the problem at hand. LoL has a single hyperparameter, the size of its global library of transformer blocks, that allows it to transition seamlessly between parameter reuse and sparsity, generalizing the parameter-efficiency and FLOPs-efficiency tradeoffs of dense, depth-recurrent, and mixture of experts architectures in one unified family. We show that LoL achieves competitive performance with all three above baselines when pretrained on text data. On synthetic sudoku and maze data, we observe that the model employs distinct layer orderings to implement different solution strategies, and we can intervene on routing to force a model to apply a specific solution strategy to a problem instance. In a multilingual natural language setting, we find that LoL chooses different layer order patterns based on language and script. Similarly, we train LoL on safety data and fit probes to its router outputs, finding that these probes distinguish safe from unsafe text with high AUROC. LoL provides a path toward a future in which models dynamically compose their own computational graph, enabling more efficient and controllable reasoning.

摘要：

##### **Harder Than the Task: From Technical to Organizational Difficulty in AI Benchmarking**
2611.03494v1 by Victor Barres, Aden Barton, Nicolai J. Foss, Henrik M. Nielsen

AI benchmarks have made remarkable progress in measuring the ability of AI systems to perform increasingly demanding cognitive and technical tasks. Yet, many of these benchmarks treat technical tasks as the unit of analysis, while much economically valuable work takes place within organizations, where tasks are embedded in a system of relations. Within organizations, successful task performance depends not only on solving the technical demands of the task but also on navigating the interdependence of activities, distributed knowledge, differentiated authority, temporal constraints, conflicting interests, and changing environmental conditions while the task is underway. Accordingly, we argue that organizational task difficulty constitutes a distinct and insufficiently recognized dimension of AI capability, and that existing approaches to benchmarking provide an incomplete basis for assessing how effectively AI agents can perform real-world organizational work. By drawing on organization theory, we develop a taxonomy of the structure of work that characterizes the conditions that agents face in organizational work. Applying this taxonomy to analyze existing benchmarks, we find limited and uneven coverage of organizational dimensions, with fewer than one quarter of prominent benchmarks claiming any of them. We therefore argue that AI benchmarks should increasingly move beyond the evaluation of technically difficult tasks toward the systematic assessment of agents' ability to navigate the organizational conditions under which economically valuable work takes place.

摘要：

##### **Playing Dumb: How a Chess Transformer Simulates Users Across Skill Levels**
2611.03439v1 by Difan Jiao, George Eilender, Zhenwei Tang, Ashton Anderson

Generative models increasingly simulate users at different skill levels in education and AI evaluation. Yet it remains unclear whether lower-skill behavior reflects less internal knowledge or a different use of retained knowledge. This distinction is difficult to study in natural language, where proficiency and relevant knowledge are hard to measure. In this work, we study Maia-2, a chess transformer that predicts human moves conditioned on player skill, using quantitative skill ratings and deterministically labeled chess concepts. We hold board positions and model weights fixed and analyze positions where skill conditioning changes the prediction from a blunder to the best move. First, we measure encoded knowledge by training probes to predict the presence of 172 chess concepts from Maia-2's internal states. Probe accuracy remains nearly constant across skill conditions, and probes trained at one level transfer accurately to the others. To test whether stronger decisions can be recovered from this retained knowledge, we fine-tune only Maia-2's final output layer, raising best-move accuracy in the lowest skill band from 14.4% to 82.8%. Training this layer only on highest-skill representations retains most of the improvement at lower levels, showing that recovery does not require direct training on lower-skill states. Steering concept-relevant features learned by a sparse autoencoder also improves lower-skill decisions while leaving probe-measured concept encoding practically unchanged. These results support an account in which skill conditioning changes how retained concept knowledge influences move selection, showing that weaker behavior can understate the knowledge available within a model.

摘要：

##### **Dhārā Bench: An Evidence-Oriented Retrieval Benchmark for Indian Knowledge Systems**
2611.03196v1 by Avinash Holenarasipur Nagendra, Anand Kaniyaar Sriranga Prasad, Kousik Sarathy Sridharan, Mohan Raghavan

Dhārā Bench is a retrieval benchmark for Indian Knowledge Systems (IKS), with 325 questions in 12 categories and 60,121 text chunks from 15 source groups. The categories were developed based on 4,500 questions submitted to the Dhārā application; the evaluation set combines corpus-aligned application queries with expert-contributed questions. The annotations comprise 7,578 question-chunk relevance links involving 6,847 distinct corpus chunks. For 34 composite and multi-hop questions, 401 conjugate groups specify alternative sets of passages that jointly support each question. Chunk identifiers and hierarchical references connect the judgments to their source texts. The paper describes corpus preparation, annotation, and validation, and reports a fixed BM25 baseline implemented with Pyserini with $k_1=0.9$ and $b=0.4$. Across all questions, BM25 achieves MRR@20 of 0.315, Recall@10 of 0.182, and nDCG@10 of 0.205. On the group-annotated subset, complete-evidence scores are CE_Recall@20 = 0.294 and CE_MRR@20 = 0.141. The category results expose particularly weak verse retrieval, while group scores reveal gaps between finding individual passages and completing their support. The corpus and evaluation code are available at https://github.com/Avinash2236/Dhara-Bench.

摘要：

##### **Toward Knowledge-Centric AI: Discovering, Internalizing, and Using Explicit Knowledge Across Agents and Generations**
2611.03157v1 by Yisen Gao, Haoyu Huang, Zhongwei Xie, Yufei Li, Hong Ting Tsang, Boyi Zhang, Qiao Xiao, Tianshi Zheng, Jiaxin Bai, Yangqiu Song

Knowledge is how understanding accumulates across people and generations: durable records that outlive their producers and that others can check, combine, teach, and correct. AI agents are now built and run by many independent users and organizations, and each agent is upgraded, replaced, or retired within months. Yet what an agent learns stays trapped in model parameters, a transient context window, or a system-specific memory store that is appended to and retrieved from rather than processed. We argue that the next generation of AI should be knowledge-centric, built around an explicit, continuously evolving knowledge layer independent of any single agent. In this loop, agents discover knowledge from their environments and trajectories; the layer internalizes it by turning fragmented, heterogeneous records and experience into composable, revisable understanding; and agents across systems and generations use the result and return new outcomes for revision. While discovery and use have advanced rapidly with large language model agents, internalization remains the bottleneck. We argue that internalization should become a persistent object of training and maintenance-centered on condition-preserving conceptualization of events and deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning across noisy, heterogeneous representations (text, tables, graphs, rules, and programs). To power this layer, we call for knowledge foundation models that provide shared neural capacity for knowledge computation, and outline a research agenda showing how an explicit knowledge layer delivers reuse, cost efficiency, auditability, and continuity across agent generations.

摘要：

##### **Preprocessing Choices in Fine-Tuning Data Matter: Utility and Demographic Sensitivity in LLM-Based Recommendation**
2611.03113v1 by Furkan Yasir Goksu, Yaren Yilmaz, Sule Gunduz Oguducu

Large language models (LLMs) have introduced a generative approach to recommendation, producing item lists from textual representations of user histories and item information. However, this generative setting raises challenges concerning output validity, prompt consistency, and sensitivity to demographic descriptors. In this work, we investigate how preprocessing choices in fine-tuning data affect recommendation utility and fairness-related behavior in a generative LLM-based recommender system. We first establish a controlled recommendation pipeline that combines supervised fine-tuning, catalog-constrained decoding, and prompt augmentation. We then study three preprocessing choices: item-title representation, demographic balancing, and auxiliary item-knowledge enrichment. Experiments on MovieLens-1M show that the catalog-constrained pipeline eliminates out-of-catalog items and ensures complete ten-item outputs, while prompt augmentation improves prompt consistency and utility. Original MovieLens titles increase HR@10 from 10.67% to 12.47% and Coverage@10 from 41.36% to 47.88%. Fairness-related effects are attribute-dependent: age balancing and knowledge enrichment reduce selected group-level utility gaps while largely preserving utility, whereas gender balancing reduces utility without improving the evaluated gender-related indicators. Overall, preprocessing choices in fine-tuning data should be evaluated jointly in terms of utility, output behavior, and demographic sensitivity.

摘要：

##### **What makes a theorem interesting? Operationalizing interestingness for open-ended exploration**
2611.02905v1 by Silvère Gangloff

An agent that explores, whether it generates conjectures or research ideas, must decide which of them are worth keeping. This judgment of interestingness is usually delegated to a hand-picked proxy or to a language model, neither of which is known to correspond to what humans actually value. I study it for mathematical theorems, by observing which results mathematicians single out in formal libraries and in papers. I model each way of singling out results as an evaluator that weights features of a result's statement, its proof and dependencies, and split its weights into a shared and a subjective part. In formalized mathematics, ex-ante features predict these selections as well as a large language-model judge, and the two complement each other. Interestingness is also largely shared. One set of weights, carried mostly by structural features such as the length of the statement and of the proof and the subfields the proof draws on, accounts for most of the signal and transfers in part across formal libraries. What remains subjective depends on practices and individual authors more than on subfields. The judge's advantage lies in its knowledge of the canon, and neither the judge nor the features rely on memory of the papers. The approach, which infers what a community finds interesting from what it singles out, can be applied to other sciences.

摘要：

##### **Bridging Low-Level Image Fusion and High-Level Semantic Understanding**
2611.02896v1 by Guangyuan Dong, Lingdong Shen, Xudong Zhang, Haodong Jing, Haoyu Wang, Zihao Li, Zonghao Ying, Ziyu Song, Shenghao Liu, Jiachen Luo, Hao Zhang

Remote sensing image fusion and semantic interpretation are usually optimized separately, so reconstruction quality need not preserve downstream retrieval utility. We propose LANCE, which uses structured language as an explicit, indexable semantic state. A frozen vision-language model maps the input to a closed-vocabulary text record; a language-conditioned generator fuses the imagery; and a dense verifier with a differentiable continuous summarizer re-describes the output. Text-text consistency and anti-collapse contrastive text-mask losses with negative queries close the loop without dense annotations or VLM fine-tuning. A variational EM formulation yields a per-step contraction result under regularity assumptions, distinguishing semantic faithfulness from verifier overfitting. Across seven test sets on GF2, QuickBird, WorldView-III, and WorldView-II, LANCE improves reduced-scale reference metrics by up to 11.7% (median 2.1%) over the strongest learning baseline. Independent evaluation using a held-out VLM and a supervised aerial detector yields gains of +6.4 mAP and +0.072 open-vocabulary F1 over the closest competitor. Text perturbations, cross-verifier transfer, and pseudo-RGB ablations examine the mechanisms underlying these gains. The framework connects image fusion to verifiable semantic representations for multimodal retrieval and knowledge management.

摘要：

##### **Think with Skills: Pseudocode Planning Before Execution for Advanced Reasoners**
2611.02830v1 by Boshen Zhou, Boxuan Yue, Xiang Li, Shanchao Yang

Language model agents often struggle with complex tasks even when equipped with expert-written skills. Such skills encode declarative knowledge, whereas successful execution requires procedural knowledge tailored to the task at hand. Natural language skills may leave conditions and dependencies implicit, and tasks involving multiple skills further require agents to coordinate intermediate results and execution order. We introduce Think with Skills, a method that trains reasoning models to turn relevant skill descriptions into task-specific pseudocode plans before execution. The pseudocode explicitly represents operations, conditions, intermediate results, and dependencies, providing a structured bridge from skill descriptions to agent actions. We construct training data that pairs tasks and skills with pseudocode plans and execution trajectories, use supervised fine tuning to initialize this behavior, and further optimize end-to-end task performance with Group Relative Policy Optimization. On SkillsBench, Think with Skills raises task success from 35.0% to 62.0%. These results show that proceduralizing expert knowledge before execution substantially improves skill use.

摘要：

##### **TRIO: Task Refinement with Individual Optimization for Retrieval-Augmented Generation**
2611.02810v1 by Yingtao Ren, Xiao Luo, Yiwei Fu, Jinzhao Zhou, Yu-Cheng Chang, Chin-Teng Lin

This paper studies retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), which leverages external knowledge to improve the performance of language models. Despite the remarkable progress, current RAG approaches are still far from satisfactory due to inadequate retrieval and potential hallucinations. Towards this end, we propose a novel approach named Task Refinement with Individual Optimization (TRIO) for RAG. ##### **Who Should Score the Cache? Mid-Depth Layers Are the Best Judges of KV-Cache Eviction**
2611.02230v1 by Ekaterina Seregina

Key-value (KV) cache eviction methods rank tokens by attention scores, and each layer scores its own cache. To our knowledge, no eviction method asks which layer should score. We find, across twenty-one models, that the depth of the scoring layer governs eviction quality. On the eighteen models with per-layer caches, spanning 0.5B to 109B, long-context retrieval accuracy collapses at shallow layers, peaks between 0.4 and 0.75 of depth, and degrades at the last layer. The curve arises because a layer judges tokens well only once it has used the question to find the answer, which takes several layers. Modeling eviction as a top-$k$ cut, we compute answer survival probability from prefill attention and reproduce the measured curves. This approach selects the scoring layer using calibration prompts with known answers, and the selected layer is the best layer measured on most models and never significantly below it. It improves on the own-layer default on sixteen of the eighteen models, with significant gains on seven, all at 14B or below, while on larger models the default is already close to the best mid-depth layer. Scoring every cache from the selected layer while keeping one set of tokens for all heads also beats per-head SnapKV by 18 to 43 points on five models up to 14B under tight budgets. This gain carries over to financial filings at 32K context, all at the same memory budget, with no extra forward pass. The same curve explains a penalty in shared-cache models, where several layers read one cache and the layer that writes it scores by default. On Gemma-4-E2B the writer is shallow and loses 28 points to a mid-depth reader at a 10% budget. We also find that in open-book question answering (LongBench) no mid-depth layer beats the own-layer default.

摘要：

##### **FOLLIE: Automated Reasoning over the First Dataset of Sentences with their First-Order Logical Representation for French**
2611.02129v1 by Maximos Skandalis

FOLLIE (for First-Order Logic for Language Inference and Entailment for French) connects French sentences, compositional first-order representations and automated natural language inference (NLI). The first French NLI-ready, NL-to-FOL resource, it contains 19,983 inference records and 36,987 non-empty sentence-slot--formula mappings from six heterogeneous NLI sources, retaining French lexical material and source problem structure. Its silver annotations are produced by a neural--symbolic pipeline, with 111 separately identified LLM-assisted additions. The construction combines morphosyntactic analysis, probability-conditioned categorial multitagging, grammatical proof search, discourse representation and first-order semantic construction. Stage-wise experiments show substantial gains in derivation coverage; analyses of conjunction distributions, logical-operator incidence and scope alternation characterise the resulting resource. We then develop an automatic inference engine whose input-conditioned background theories address lexical relations, quantification, quantities, event structure and temporal dependencies. Prover9 supplies proofs for entailment and contradiction, while Mace4 supports bounded consistency checks. Three-way accuracies are 65.37% on all 335 FraCaS-FR problems, 70.33% on 300 GQNLI-FR problems and 71.35% on 9,399 eligible SICK-FR records pooled across splits. Agreement with reference labels among committed decisions is 93.53%, 100.00% and 98.66%, at commitment coverage of 41.49%, 37.00% and 15.05%, respectively. Worked constructions, axiom schemas and proof analyses explain these outcomes and identify the contributions of scope, reference, lexical knowledge and numerical structure. FOLLIE provides a French experimental basis for studying the representations and assumptions that connect sentence interpretation to deductive inference.

摘要：

##### **Order-Only On-Policy Distillation**
2611.02112v1 by Guangzeng Han, Yu Wu, Xiaolei Huang

On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own generations by matching the teacher's token probabilities. These probabilities entangle two kinds of information: which tokens the teacher prefers, and how confident it is. The latter reflects the teacher's own capacity and calibration, which a smaller student may be unable to reproduce. Because the teacher temperature changes these values without changing the ordering, OPD is also sensitive to the temperature choice. We ask whether the teacher's ordering of candidate tokens is enough, and propose Order-Only On-Policy Distillation (O$^{3}$PD). At each student-generated prefix, O$^{3}$PD trains the student to reproduce the order of the teacher's top-$K$ tokens, without fitting their probability values. The objective is therefore invariant to the teacher temperature and needs no temperature tuning. Across six teacher--student pairs, O$^{3}$PD outperforms OPD on the average over HMMT25, AIME24, and AIME25 in every pair, by up to 2.9 points, with gains of up to 5.6 points on AIME25. Other ranking losses yield similar gains, so the benefit comes from ordinal supervision itself. Analysis shows that O$^{3}$PD departs further from the teacher's probabilities than OPD, yet reproduces its ranking more faithfully and yields a stronger student. The ranking, not the probability values, thus carries the knowledge that on-policy distillation needs to transfer.

##### **Decoupled Predictive Decision Making: A Shared Conditional Predictor for Agents**
2611.02075v1 by Lang Mo, Xingxuan Zhang, Qi Wang, Mingchao Hao, Hao Yuan, Chunjing Gan, Wang Lu, Peng Cui

Agent decisions depend on empirical relationships that deployment observations reveal and task descriptions alone may leave unresolved. We introduce Decoupled Predictive Decision Making (DPDM), an architecture that formulates heterogeneous agent decisions as conditional prediction with a shared pretrained learner. Language models provide semantic representations of current and historical records, while a general-purpose conditional predictor predicts task-defined targets from the represented evidence. Pretraining supplies reusable inference capability; deployment evidence instantiates problem-specific predictions without updating either model's neural parameters. Tool judgments, candidate preferences, and reasoning assessments share an evidence-and-query interface, with predictions mapped to choices by a decision rule. This architecture establishes complementary sources of agent capability: semantic knowledge acquired through language pretraining and empirical inference acquired through structured-data pretraining. A single frozen LimiX-2 predictor serves all five task categories. With Qwen3.5-27B as the semantic encoder, DPDM raises mean accuracy across 50 benchmarks from 78.80% with few-shot prompting to 84.92% under their respective evaluated history budgets, and reaches 82.33% with identical histories and queries. On ToolPRMBench's BFCL and ToolSandbox tasks, absolute accuracy gains are 34.26% and 28.87%, respectively, at the methods' respective evaluated history budgets. Accuracy improves as history grows. With identical imbalanced histories, mean balanced accuracy improves by an absolute 14.51% across five reasoning-step assessment tasks and four encoders. DPDM makes learning from experience an independently improvable foundation-model capability, creating a shared route for advances in conditional prediction to improve decisions across agent tasks.

##### **State-Aware Virtual Patient Simulation for Training Psychiatrists in Challenging Clinical Scenarios**
2611.02067v1 by Harsh Mohta, C Chandrahas Reddy, Sunhith Reddy Bojja, Manik Inder Singh Sethi, Vinesh Gupta, Raghuram Bharadwaj Diddigi

Psychiatrists must be prepared to manage patients exhibiting challenging psychological states, including suicidal ideation, hallucinations, and other high-risk behaviors. However, opportunities to safely practice such interactions are limited. While recent conversational agents can simulate patients, existing approaches largely rely on large language models (LLMs) to determine patient responses, making it difficult to consistently maintain psychological state, adapt to the clinician's responses, and generate clinically plausible challenges that meaningfully test the clinician. We present a state-aware virtual patient simulation pipeline for training psychiatrists in challenging clinical scenarios. The proposed system maintains an evolving patient state throughout the interaction and orchestrates patient behavior rather than leaving response generation entirely to an LLM. At each interaction, a learning-based behavioral strategy selector determines the type and level of challenge appropriate to the current patient and clinician context. The selected strategy, patient state, clinician response, interaction history, and relevant clinical knowledge are then provided as grounded context to an LLM to generate a semantically relevant and psychologically consistent patient response. Following each session, the system analyzes the interaction and provides feedback to the clinician on their communication and management of the virtual patient.

##### **Do Embodied VLMs Actually Use Their Scene-Graph Memory?**
2611.02056v1 by Shubh Garg, Divyansh Pandey, Vedant Khandelwal, Niyati Rawal, Amit Sheth

Embodied agents that answer questions about their route need a memory of what they saw, and scene graphs are a natural form for it. A vision-language model (VLM) can receive such a graph as text or through a graph encoder, but accuracy alone cannot show whether the graph holds the episode's facts, reaches the model, or is actually relied on. In this diagnostic study, we introduce a graph-swap test for the last of these: each episode is given another episode's graph, and we count how many answers change. On an embodied question-answering benchmark with routes through two to seven rooms, we use it to compare ways of delivering scene-graph memory to a fine-tuned VLM. As text, the memory is used strongly, adding 30 points on route questions. As a graph, it is not, and we trace why: the encoder preserves the information, but standard pooled and late fusion discard it. Giving each node its own input token recovers part of it, a gain of 8.3 points on route questions that the swap test confirms comes from the graph. The test also exposes a shortcut that accuracy hides: when the same facts appear in the text, the model ignores its graph. The test thus offers a cheap check of whether a VLM uses its scene-graph memory, and our analysis points to how that memory should be delivered.

##### **CULTURE-CMT: Benchmarking Contextualized Social Media Translation**
2611.02018v1 by Linjuan Wu, Dingming Li, Yiwen Qiu, Xinze Lyu, Ye Guo, Daoxin Zhang, Zhe Xu, Weiming Lu

Social-media comments are interactions, yet they are often translated and evaluated as isolated text. This mismatch is especially problematic when meaning depends on surrounding discussion, community-specific slang, or culturally situated expressions. We introduce CULTURE-CMT, a Chinese-English benchmark for contextualized social-media comment translation that restores the post and conversational context naturally available to users. Its 572 manually verified examples are organized into Slang, Context, and Both, enabling diagnostic analysis of distinct and interacting sources of translation difficulty. To support scalable evaluation, we further develop a retrieval-augmented cultural-effectiveness Judger that combines a calibrated scoring model with dynamically retrieved cultural knowledge. CULTURE-CMT reveals several non-trivial patterns. Providing interaction context improves most model configurations, but does not eliminate culturally ineffective translations. Examples requiring both contextual grounding and culturally situated language remain substantially more difficult than either source of difficulty alone. Moreover, larger models are not consistently better, and thinking produces highly model-dependent gains, including occasional degradation. In contrast, a 27B model adapted on benchmark-disjoint social-media data achieves the lowest observed invalidity rate among the evaluated configurations under thinking inference with context. Together, these results suggest that realistic social-media translation is not simply a matter of scaling models or reasoning longer, but of learning to translate meaning as it is constructed within evolving online interactions.

##### **MedPsy: State-of-the-Art Small Medical Language Models for Efficient Edge Deployment**
2611.02011v1 by Davide Vitabile, Alexandro Buffa, Akshay Nambiar, Amril Nazir

Medical language models promise expert clinical support, but the strongest open-source LLMs remain too large for private edge-device deployment, while compact medical models still trail substantially on knowledge-intensive and real-world healthcare tasks. Reasoning models that fit edge memory budgets can produce long reasoning traces, increasing sequential decoding, end-to-end latency, energy consumption, and KV-cache memory pressure. We present MedPsy, a family of text-only 1.7B and 4B medical language models designed for edge deployment, targeting this accuracy-generation-efficiency trade-off. Our recipe combines a synthetic medical data pipeline over biology, medicine, and health seeds with chain-of-thought targets from a 235B medical-focused LLM teacher; a four-stage post-training curriculum comprising two SFT stages followed by two RL stages with hard-sample mining; and an edge deployment evaluation spanning multiple GGUF quantization variants and real-device inference on laptop and smartphone hardware. On seven closed-ended medical benchmarks, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-1.5-4B by 19.34 points and matches MedGemma-27B while being 6.75x smaller; MedPsy-1.7B outperforms MedGemma-1.5-4B by 11.42 points despite being less than half its size. On HealthBench-Hard, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-27B by 15.33 points, while MedPsy-1.7B surpasses it by 11.66 points despite being ~16x smaller. Beyond accuracy, MedPsy reduces average response length by 1.7x (1.7B) and 3.2x (4B) versus its Qwen3 backbones, while Q4_K_M quantization preserves the aggregate score within 1 point of BF16 with ~69% lower disk footprint. In real-device inference, MedPsy-4B's shorter generations translate into 75% lower median end-to-end latency and estimated energy per answer than its Qwen3 backbone at matched quantization, while peak RAM remains below 5 GB across our tested laptop and smartphone configurations.

##### **Retrieval-Enhanced Cultural Evaluation for Social Media Translation Training**
2611.02009v1 by Linjuan Wu, Yiwen Qiu, Xinze Lyu, Dingming Li, Hui Yao, Zhongyi Sang, Ye Guo, Daoxin Zhang, Zhe Xu, Fei zhao, Mu Chuan, Yao Hu, Weiming Lu, Yongliang Shen
