##### **Knowledge-Guided Floodwater Level Reasoning for Cloud-Edge Collaborative Monitoring**
2611.01970v1 by Zhengshu Zhou, Shaoyu Wang, Ziyi Geng, Lu Tao, Qian Long

Frequent urban floodwater poses a serious threat to the safety and efficiency of intelligent transportation systems. Existing vision-based methods for floodwater level detection face two main limitations. First, the lack of explicit reasoning leads to poor detection performance and little interpretability. Second, these methods struggle to generalize to the complex and varied real-world road scenes seen from different viewpoints. To address this, we reformulate the task as an explicit visual reasoning problem and propose HydroReasoner, a floodwater level reasoning framework designed for cloud-edge collaboration. HydroReasoner builds on Qwen2.5-VL and is trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization. To address the scarcity of reasoning process annotations, we build a floodwater domain knowledge graph HydroKG and design a novel KG path reward. Through deterministic graph traversal over HydroKG, this reward supervises the model's reasoning process at a fine granularity, which improves both reasoning and generalization. For edge deployment, we apply 4-bit quantization to the model, cutting GPU memory usage by 57% and inference latency by 43% while out-of-domain accuracy drops by only 0.2 percentage points. We also propose a confidence-based cloud-edge routing mechanism, which improves system safety and eases the computational load on the cloud. In addition, we build HydroLevel, a floodwater benchmark dataset of 3,000 images, for training and evaluating the model. Experimental results show that HydroReasoner achieves detection accuracy 4.0 and 13.6 percentage points higher than conventional vision baselines in the in-domain and out-of-domain evaluations respectively, with performance dropping by only 4.4 percentage points out of domain.

##### **Launder-Bench: Can Repository-Level Agentic Code Laundering Be Detected?**
2611.01966v1 by Yuntong Hu, Hongji Pu, Zimo Li, Ruomeng Li, Xiaotong Liu, Ziyuan Qin, Qiyue Li, Liang Zhao

Coding agents can now rewrite repository-scale codebases while preserving tested behavior, creating a new challenge for plagiarism detection, license auditing, and software provenance. We ask whether such rewrites can still be traced to their source. Existing clone benchmarks emphasize fragments and bounded edits, while repository-transformation benchmarks evaluate functional correctness rather than provenance. Moreover, ranking the most similar source does not establish derivation when independent implementations can be similarly close. We define repository-level agentic code laundering and introduce Launder-Bench, to our knowledge the first repository-level benchmark for provenance under agentic rewriting, constructed from 282 real-world repositories. A source-hidden reconstruction protocol decomposes source behavior, reconstructs modules and repository structure, and retains rewrites only after execution-based verification. We evaluate the same provenance problem in two complementary settings: Launder-Bench-Pair contains 1,128 pairwise instances asking whether two repositories share provenance, while Launder-Bench-Attr contains 229 source-attribution instances asking which candidate is the source, with abstention when evidence is insufficient. Measured same-domain independent repositories establish how much similarity can arise without shared provenance and provide detector-specific reference bands. Existing methods remain far from saturating Launder-Bench: the strongest static method reaches only 0.66 AUROC on pairwise detection, while repository encoders rank the true source first on up to 22% of attribution queries but earn calibrated attribution credit on only 1-2%. These results reveal a systematic gap between relative code similarity and source-specific provenance evidence, providing a reproducible target for provenance detection under agentic rewriting.

##### **Hippocampal consolidation without replay in fully fine-tuned networks**
2611.01952v1 by Keuntae Kim, Yong Suk Choi

Networks that keep learning overwrite what they know. Prevailing remedies replay stored data, distil from old models or freeze the backbone. The brain suggests a different division of labour: the hippocampus indexes where experience lies in cortex while the cortex keeps learning. We show that this division lets a fully trainable network keep learning without cross-task replay, a teacher or inference-time memory. After each task, a training-only hippocampal memory indexes the input geometry of every group of synapses sharing an input, residual energy included, and limits each synapse's movement per direction before and after the optimizer. This class-free geometry carries most of the protection; with conflict detection on top, the method cuts absorption of established classes by near-duplicates several-fold against every compared method. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of one principle keeping whole networks learning across four architecture families and models up to 8 billion parameters. Methods that freeze the backbone hold up only as far as its representation already separates the classes; here the whole network keeps learning to tell near-duplicates apart, leading the frozen-backbone baseline by 17.5 points on TACRED. Without the memory, much old knowledge stays decodable but beyond the network's reach.

##### **VLSplat: Vision-Language Guided Object-Centric 3D Gaussian Splatting via Scene Graph**
2611.01939v1 by Yuntae Jeon, Younho Jeon, Sujin Jin, Sungho Jo, Seunghee Park

Learning semantic representations for 3D Gaussian Splatting has recently emerged as a promising direction for 3D scene understanding. However, existing 2D-to-3D semantic lifting approaches rely primarily on 2D segmentation supervision and lack object-level priors, causing the learned representation to be driven by local mask evidence rather than holistic object structure. In this paper, we present VLSplat, a vision-language guided approach for object-centric semantic lifting in 3D Gaussian Splatting. Our method augments mask-based lifting with object-level semantic priors derived from vision-language models and refines Gaussian representations using an object-group scene graph. The refinement is performed through two language-guided modules: intra-group refinement suppresses semantic noise within object groups, while inter-group acquisition expands object support regions by incorporating structurally relevant candidates from neighboring groups. Extensive experiments on indoor scene datasets demonstrate that VLSplat improves the semantic consistency and structural completeness of object-level categories. Our code and models are available at https://vlsplat.github.io/.

##### **TriPhysBench: Evaluating Physical Intelligence for Embodied Agents**
2611.01934v1 by Zhongxiang Sun, Chenghao Yang, Haojie Ding, Boheng Zhang, Fan Yang, Qingsong Wen, Kun Wang

Embodied agents need physical intelligence to understand their surroundings through physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning. These capabilities must work together to interpret events and make quantitative judgments. Existing benchmarks advance physical understanding and general-purpose tool use, yet the coordination of these capabilities in physical scenarios remains insufficiently characterized. Inspired by Popper's Three Worlds, we introduce TriPhysBench, a benchmark of 410 questions from 200 videos that brings physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning into a shared evaluation setting. Its measurement-grounded and knowledge-grounded subtasks impose complementary demands on obtaining observations and acquiring external specifications, then applying them to quantitative problems. Among six representative vision-language models, the best achieves an answer accuracy of only 50.38%, revealing limited performance on tasks that require these capabilities to work together. We further introduce TriPhys, a framework that learns a unified policy for physical perception, knowledge retrieval, and quantitative reasoning from complete tool-integrated trajectories. The policy uses intermediate reasoning to guide retrieval and computation, then incorporates tool feedback into subsequent physical inference. On 110 questions from videos excluded from training, TriPhys improves answer and solution scores across three Qwen configurations, with a 17.7% relative gain in answer accuracy for TriPhys-27B over its Qwen3.6-27B backbone. ##### **Geometric Morphology Learning: Empowering Foundation Models from Structure to Spacetime Intelligence**
2611.01932v1 by Li Sun, Philip S. Yu

Geometry is the very form that connects space, time, and matter, serving as the fundamental fabric of the physical world, from continuous fields to discrete structures. Despite its importance, deep learning is inherently blind to geometry. Riemannian geometry stands as a well-established mathematical foundation for studying geometry, yet it remains largely incompatible with deep learning architectures. To bridge this gap, we establish a new learning framework--Geometric Morphology Learning--that learns geometric or topological structures, shapes or conformations for representation learning, reasoning and action modeling. Moving beyond early attempts limited to specific geometric priors, this theory centers on modeling the intrinsic geometry of objects and learning from such geometric or topological knowledge. Specifically, we construct a neural bundle, a unified scaffolding linking local and global structures, and formulate differentiable operators, including connection and curvature, thereby enabling the study of geometry with advanced geometric concepts such as holonomy and cohomology. Consequently, Geometric Morphology Learning opens new horizons in foundation models, reasoning and planning, graph modeling, physical simulation, PDE systems, and dynamics, marking a fundamental shift toward instilling systematic geometry in artificial intelligence.

##### **RECEIPT: Measuring and Repairing the Return Assumption of LLM Agents in Web Service Operations**
2611.01930v1 by Boyang Yang, Ziyao Yang, Yu Zhang, Duo Chai, Xiaoming Chen, Haoye Tian

LLM agents operate Web infrastructure from the command line and rely on a return assumption, namely that each tool call returns within a bounded time and with its requested action finished. Many commands, however, ask a question before they act, and the agent's harness either closes the program's input or keeps it open as a terminal at which nobody types. With closed input, the program can return status 0 without the requested action. With open input, it waits until a time limit, and the agent pays in time and tokens. To the best of our knowledge, this paper is the first to measure and repair the return assumption. We collect 91,469 production tool calls and 89,047 public agent trajectories, test how 8 widely used harnesses launch commands, and build Litmus, a catalog of 158 checked command-line invocations, and Tripwire, a benchmark of 25 task pairs that differ only in whether a question guards the requested action. We then propose RECEIPT, which keeps the input open, reads what each process waits for from system calls, and returns this state to an unmodified agent through a model-checked protocol.
  On Litmus, closed input causes 11.5% of tested invocations to return status 0 without performing the requested action, and 7 of 8 harnesses close input when no terminal is attached. An unanswered question at a plain terminal multiplies a task's token use by 2.9, and the input connection moves an LLM from 7th to 3rd place among 8. Across 5 LLMs, RECEIPT reduces false claims of success by 73.5% and completes all 25 guarded tasks on a second harness. An agent's reliability therefore depends on what its harness lets it observe as well as on its LLM. Agent builders should report what each tool call did, not only how it ended, and benchmark maintainers should fix and report the input connection before they rank LLMs.

##### **TAC-CoT: Tool-Empowered Implicit Chain-of-Thought via Interleaving Explicit Tokens**
2611.01926v1 by Juseon Do, Sangmin Bae, Jeesu Jung, Hwanjun Song

LLM-based agentic systems are increasingly combined with CoT reasoning to solve tasks that require accurate reasoning, external knowledge, and multi-step deliberation. Yet, explicit CoT incurs substantial generation overhead, while implicit CoT, which replaces textual rationales with compact continuous tokens, does not naturally support the explicit communication interface required for agentic tool-use. We propose TAC-CoT, a tool-empowered adaptive implicit CoT framework that replaces explicit reasoning traces with compact latent reasoning segments while retaining textual tokens only for tool calls, observations, and final answers. This interleaved design enables agentic tool-use without verbalizing every intermediate reasoning step. Experiments on agentic math and QA benchmarks show that TAC-CoT improves accuracy by up to 8.8 points while reducing model forward passes by up to 6.8x. ##### **Rethinking Model Aggregation for Federated Learning with Domain Skew: A Knowledge-Preserving Perspective**
2611.01893v1 by Haotian Wu, Xu Ji, Qiheng Li, Li Xiong, Zibin Pan

Domain skew challenges federated learning by requiring a shared model to accommodate distinct visual distributions. A full-duplex model should answer easy questions immediately, but hard questions often need stronger reasoning, which may require waiting. ##### **DyRadio: Spatiotemporal Diffusion for Dynamic Radio Map Reconstruction**
2611.01603v1 by Zhijie Fan, Zhifeng Zhao, Rongpeng Li

Accurate radio map (RM) reconstruction is essential for environment-aware wireless networks. Sampling-based methods, which infer radio conditions from sparse measurements, are widely adopted because they do not require explicit knowledge of transmitter configurations. However, in dynamic environments, time-varying propagation conditions cause RMs to evolve, complicating reconstruction from temporally distributed sparse observations. Although cross-frame observations provide complementary spatial information, their direct aggregation can introduce temporal state mismatch. Furthermore, unknown measurement noise adds to the reconstruction difficulties. To address these challenges, we propose DyRadio, a framework that integrates spatiotemporal RM reconstruction with unknown noise estimation. Specifically, a propagation-prior-guided, noise-conditioned spatiotemporal diffusion model (DM) jointly reconstructs consecutive RMs by capturing cross-frame dependencies, while adapting to measurement noise levels. To estimate the noise level, an iterative predictive likelihood method alternates between DM-based blind-area prediction and noise-level updates. The estimated noise level then conditions reconstruction of the complete RM sequence. Experimental results demonstrate superior reconstruction accuracy and robustness against measurement noise, with peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) improvements of 1.18-5.75 dB over the best-performing baseline across all evaluated settings.

##### **Search-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation with Sibling Alignment for Generative Recommendation**
2611.01568v1 by Rongfeng Guo, Zhuang Zhuang, Ji Dai, Peng Zhao, Shijie Li, Jie Chen, Teng Long, Vincent Tao Hu, Fei Pan

Generative recommendation retrieves items by autoregressively generating semantic IDs, typically via beam search. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) enhances training supervision by transferring privileged self-teacher knowledge at student-generated states. However, beam-search retention depends on competition among prefixes: visited states differ in importance, and initially reliable decisions may approach the pruning boundary as training evolves. Moreover, conventional distribution matching couples target-token confidence with preferences among competing continuations, potentially weakening relational supervision when the self-teacher strongly favors the target. To address these challenges, we propose Search-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation (SA-OPSD), a framework that transfers privileged self-teacher knowledge to the states visited during the student's own beam search. Specifically, we introduce Evolving-Boundary Sampling, which prioritizes search-critical states by considering both current pruning-boundary proximity and initial-reference retention-margin decline under a controlled distillation budget. Furthermore, we develop Confidence-Decoupled Branch Distillation, which separates target-probability calibration from informative sibling relations to better preserve the teacher's preferences among competing branches. Experiments on Industrial, Office, and Instruments demonstrate strong performance for SA-OPSD.

##### **Write Facts the Way Models Read Them: Lifting the Two-Hop Curse**
2611.01519v1 by Zhiyun Shi

A language model that learns a new fact by fine-tuning can repeat it but cannot reason with it: taught that a person is a citizen of a new country, it still cannot say, without chain-of-thought, who the head of state of that person's country is. This two-hop curse is widely treated as a reasoning deficit, and the strongest knowledge editors answer it with synthesized reasoning data. We show that the reasoning is intact and the writing is wrong. Fine-tuning writes a new fact as an answer to be said, whereas the second hop reads the bridge ("that person's country") as an entity, from the middle layers on: patching the model's own representation of the entity into the bridge at those layers largely restores the second hop, yet after fine-tuning the bridge there is no closer to the new entity than before the edit. We therefore propose read-equivalent writing (Legible): one loss term makes the bridge phrase, at the layer where the second hop finishes reading it, match the model's own reading of the new entity, and keeps the same relation's other subjects unchanged. Phrases and targets come from the edited model itself, and no multi-hop training data is used. Across Qwen2.5, Llama-3 and Qwen3, Legible (I) composes learned facts more often than the model composes its own knowledge, on MQuAKE's two-hop-curse questions; (II) reaches 1.8-2.6x the bridge accuracy of CaKE, whose reasoning data composes no better than plain fine-tuning, and has the best average over composition, accuracy and locality against four families of writers; and (III) lifts the curse on RippleEdits, on the benchmark of Balesni et al., who named the curse, and at 14B.

##### **FedUDG: Federated Domain Generalization with Unlabeled Clients**
2611.01498v1 by Junyu Ren, Wensheng Gan, Philip S. Yu

Federated domain generalization (FDG) aims to learn models that generalize reliably to out-of-distribution (OOD) environments without centralizing client's data. However, existing FDG methods assume that all source clients have labeled data, limiting their ability to leverage the abundant unlabeled client data available in practice. To address this limitation, we propose FedUDG. At the federation level, FedUDG utilizes a parameter-functional differentiation strategy that maintains a semantic anchor updated solely using ground-truth labels. The remaining parameters absorb knowledge from unlabeled domains under bounded aggregation, preventing domain-biased noise from eroding the class semantics. Within each unlabeled client, FedUDG utilizes a student-teacher framework with two complementary objectives: (1) semantic self-training on high-confidence samples, using the functional differentiation between the semantic anchor and classifier to constrain the model to learn semantically pure and cross-domain stable features; and (2) label-free representation distillation on all unlabeled samples to stabilize learning under appearance variations. Together, these objectives guide the model toward a transferable feature space, while mitigating overfitting to domain-specific characteristics. Furthermore, to address the lack of systematic evaluation of FDG under formal privacy constraints, we develop a sample-level differentially private extension, DP-FedUDG, to quantify the trade-off between privacy and OOD generalization. Across four evaluation protocols, FedUDG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/Jr10426/FedUDG

##### **Can Large Language Model Agents Learn by Watching Tutorial Videos?**
2611.01460v1 by Haoyu Zhao, Zafeirios Fountas, Jun Wang

Humans continuously acquire knowledge from carefully crafted materials such as books and tutorial videos, carrying that understanding into future tasks. Millions of such instructional videos are uploaded online every day, yet LLM agents remain unable to exploit this ever-expanding wealth of human teaching. Their capabilities are frozen after training, while parameter updates at scale remain prohibitively expensive. This raises the central question: can LLM agents learn from this vast instructional resource without parameter updates? We introduce Tutorial Agentic Learning (TAL), a nonparametric continual-learning paradigm that consolidates tutorial videos into a persistent, structured tutorialbook memory through a two-stage adversarial process that harvests hard, video-grounded questions and uses them to refine the memory. At inference time, TAL retrieves through a Table-of-Contents (TOC) tree, tying retrieval directly to the model's reasoning rather than to embedding similarity. To study this setting, we introduce VidLearn, a multimodal QA benchmark built from 72 chess tutorial videos spanning 12 openings. On VidLearn, TAL outperforms all evaluated baselines and improves monotonically as more videos are consumed, achieving up to 113% and 91% relative gains over the corresponding vanilla MLLMs on the training and test splits, respectively. In actual gameplay, TAL reaches a stronger opening-phase position than its vanilla counterpart in 69% of head-to-head games. Evaluation in a second domain further demonstrates the generality of TAL. Together, these results highlight instructional videos as a scalable resource for parameter-update-free agent continual learning.

##### **Vocabulary Aligned Explanations for Computer Vision Models**
2611.01391v1 by Erfan Mirhaji, Nikhil Krishnaswamy, Jill Zarestky, Lisa Mason, Nathaniel Blanchard, Sarath Sreedharan

Optimizing vision models for accuracy does not force the model to learn and utilize human-understandable and domain-specific concepts and rules. We propose explaining image classifications through domain-specific concepts, visual evidence, and expert knowledge expressed as logical rules. We introduce FLUENT (Framework for Localized, Uncertainty-Aware Explanations in Normative Terminology), a post-hoc framework that ranks expert-authored rules combining jointly required, alternative, and absent concepts. To account for prediction errors, FLUENT combines predicted labels and concept states with empirically estimated likelihoods and concept priors. It scores each rule by calculating support over satisfying assignments to the concepts mentioned in that rule, then normalizes these scores to express relative confidence among candidate explanations. The selected rule is illustrated through occlusion-based highlights for positive concept mentions in the query image and localized examples in retrieved reference images for negative mentions. These illustrations are intended to show end users unfamiliar with the domain what the concepts named in an explanation look like. FLUENT combines domain knowledge and model accuracy, and delivers visual concept highlights and explicit rule confidence to explain an image in the vocabulary of an application domain.

##### **Geometry of Confabulation and Refusal in Small Language Models**
2611.01272v1 by Vladimir Kazei

Faced with an unanswerable question, small language models can either refuse or confabulate: confidently produce a wrong answer. Can the hidden state before the first generated token provide a clue? On Qwen3-1.7B and Gemma 2 2B the probe separates upcoming refusals from confabulations well beyond prompt features (+7.4/+16.3 pp); on Llama 3.2 3B the ConfabQA margin is within fold-to-fold spread, but a direct refusal probe on PopQA and TriviaQA recovers +18-25 pp. On Qwen I closely inspect three candidate directions for the refusal vector: probe-recovered, Qwen-Scope SAE feature, and directly optimized. Although the three directions are pairwise 50-81 degrees apart, a push at or below the state's own norm along any of them flips most confabulations into refusal openings dominated by the "As..." family. Forcing the opener without touching the hidden state yields the same one-in-three judged-refusal rate as any push. The opposite push collapses refusals mostly into confabulations, yet leaves originally correct answers intact, so the direction gates the opening speech act rather than the knowledge behind it. Correctness signal is model-dependent and portable only for Llama 3.2 3B: 81% of the probe's margin over test majority survives transfer between ConfabQA, PopQA, and TriviaQA. Benchmark, judge, and code are released.

##### **Compile as Hypothesis: A Headroom Typology for Prompt Optimization on Real Reasoning Models**
2611.01135v1 by Zhengshuai Yang, Changyun Li

Automated prompt compilers search for or rewrite prompts under the implicit assumption that optimization consistently improves downstream task performance. Across four optimizer families (genome search, profile-guided mutation, GEPA, and ESPO) and eight tasks, structured search yields no statistically significant accuracy gains on strong reasoners. However, a discrete structural genome provides essential architectural stability, preventing catastrophic bimodal seed-collapse (where unconstrained search collapses toward the judge floor in 3/5 runs). Through 190-problem evaluations on knowledge-intensive benchmarks, we establish a three-dimensional headroom typology: prompt optimization cannot compensate for intrinsic knowledge deficits or physical token truncation, but successfully resolves protocol and formatting errors. On gpt-6, while free-text reflective search (GEPA) yields a +.14 gain on a task where structured search remains flat, qualitative ablation reveals that ~.035 stems from judge-rubric reconstruction while ~.105 reflects genuine instruction refinement. Our pre-registered AutoAPC-Select deployment gate matches the oracle policy in 5/7 groups and rescues seed-collapse arms with zero regret. However, a fast, well-formed decision does not show that the model used the evidence. We introduce S1CogBench to ask what these models can and cannot do on 24 cognitive tasks, evaluating five open models and Jev and keeping conditions and scoring apart from disclosed adaptations. We find that decision models vary widely in reliability, acting well does not imply predicting humans, aggregate scores hide how decisions respond to conditions, and speed comes mainly from answering without generation. To our knowledge, S1CogBench is the first benchmark of System One decision models on established cognitive experiments, and it gives a shared test of whether their decisions respond to evidence, feedback, memory load, and future opportunities before software relies on them.

##### **Adaptive-Policy Distillation: Knowledge Injection under Information Asymmetry**
2611.00852v1 by Hojun Cho, Kang Eun Jeon, Jaegul Choo

On-policy distillation (OPD), which uses reverse-KL supervision on student rollouts to transfer a context-conditioned model's behavior into student model parameters for closed-book inference, has shown strong results. However, we find that this success does not translate to knowledge injection: across two document-QA benchmarks, OPD injects document knowledge far less efficiently than simple off-policy distillation. We trace this reversal to two failures that are structural, yet sparse across token positions. Reverse KL weights its gradient by the student distribution and therefore barely lifts document-specific tokens the student considers unlikely; after forward KL restores this signal, student rollouts expose later positions on the target trajectory only after generating the preceding tokens correctly, so coverage deteriorates with target length. Motivated by the concentration of teacher-student divergence at these hard positions, we propose Adaptive-Policy Distillation (APD), whose per-token gate jointly shifts the rollout toward the teacher and the objective toward forward KL where needed, withdrawing both interventions as the student learns. Without labels or a verifier, APD improves over OPD by up to 54.6%, achieving the best scores of 0.558 F1 on QASPER and 0.486 MRR on BioASQ.

##### **Can Agentic AI Undermine Its Own Assurance Loop? Closed-Loop Dependency Assurance for Cognitive Network Digital Twins**
2611.00838v1 by Ahmed Hussain, Konstantinos Kalogiannis, Salahuddin Salahuddin, Hexu Li, Thiemo Voigt

Agentic Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Network Digital Twins (NDTs) are increasingly combined to form Cognitive Digital Twins (CDTs) that translate operational intents into network changes and support closed-loop management. However, a network change may achieve its intended objective while disrupting the monitoring or recovery mechanisms needed for subsequent decisions. For example, a service may be rerouted while its latency-monitoring traffic continues along the original path, producing measurements that no longer reflect the service's performance. We propose Closed-Loop Dependency Assurance (CLDA), an assurance mechanism that augments agentic AI-enabled NDTs with dependency-aware validation. In our framework, an agentic AI planner generates and refines network plans, while the NDT evaluates the projected network outcomes of these plans. CLDA extends this analysis using a Loop Dependency Graph (LDG) that connects service requirements to monitoring mechanisms, forwarding paths, failure domains, and recovery prerequisites. CLDA projects candidate actions onto these dependencies, checks intermediate states of multistep plans, and supports deterministic make-before-break repairs using temporary monitoring mechanisms. Unlike the Large Language Model (LLM)-based planner, CLDA uses explicit, deterministic rules for assurance decisions and repair. We evaluate CLDA through controlled reconfigurations, a 240-trial LLM-based planning campaign, and live network execution. A separate synthetic study examines deterministic assurance scalability across network models representing up to 1,024 routers.

##### **Online Fair Division of Chores with Upgrade Recourse**
2611.00432v1 by Yichen Huang, Nicholas Teh

We study the online fair division of indivisible chores among $n$ agents under upgrade recourse, where earlier assignments may be revised but each chore previously assigned to an agent must be replaced by a distinct chore that she finds no more costly. We show that a simple adaptation of round robin maintains envy-freeness up to one chore (EF1) together with a $(2-1/n)$-approximation of the maximin share (MMS), and that no deterministic algorithm without predictions can do better, even when all agents rank the chores in the same order. This stands in contrast to the allocation of goods, where the optimal MMS factor vanishes as $n$ grows and cannot be achieved together with EF1. On the other hand, envy-freeness up to any chore (EFX) essentially requires the exact number of chores: uncertainty between two consecutive values already rules out every finite approximation, and even exact knowledge does not allow EFX to be combined with an MMS guarantee better than the trivial factor of $n$.

##### **Unlearning by Extrapolating a Shrink-and-Heal Lookahead**
2611.00374v1 by Hoang-Chau Luong, Lingwei Chen

Unlearning aims to make a large language model behave like one retrained without its forget set. Our key idea is to shrink the fine-tuned model toward its pre-fine-tuning weights and briefly re-learn the retain set, thereby removing the forget-specific contribution; in the linearized regime, this shrink-and-heal lookahead moves the model a fixed fraction of the way to the retrained model, its unique fixed point. ##### **Automated Theorem Generation in First-Order Logic by Construction from Triangular Standard Contradictions**
2611.00269v1 by Yang Xu, Shuwei Chen, Hailin Guo, Jun Liu

Automated theorem generation (ATG) seeks to construct new theorems rather than merely prove given conjectures. We propose a constructive framework for ATG in first-order logic based on triangular standard contradictions. The method constructs minimally unsatisfiable clause sets from selected first-order literals, then forms theorems by retaining some clauses as premises and taking the negation of one clause, or a conjunction of clauses, as the conclusion. Minimal unsatisfiability ensures that the remaining premises are satisfiable, avoiding trivial inference by explosion, while unsatisfiability of the full set guarantees the resulting entailment. Theorems are therefore correct by construction, without a separate proof procedure for each instance. Extending the previously developed $Δ_1$ generator, we introduce seven methods, $Δ_2$--$Δ_8$, forming a unified family of eight theorem-generation methods. Four generators, $Δ_1$--$Δ_4$, have been implemented and released. We also formulate a general $Δ$-class method encompassing the family. Combinatorial analysis demonstrates the capacity to generate large numbers of distinct theorems systematically. This framework offers a principled foundation for automated theorem discovery and the construction of logically guaranteed knowledge.

The pipeline is demonstrated on a relation extraction task applied to popular science articles in English, targeting four ontologically-grounded relation types (strong causation, weak causation, contrastive, and compositional), with semantic node canonicalisation and interactive, colour-coded HTML causal graphs as the final output. Performance is evaluated through an anonymous user study, achieving an average perceived accuracy of 0.778 against a human-annotated gold standard. The modular architecture supports flexible deployment across both API-based and in-house LLM setups, and the full framework is released under an open license to foster reproducibility and collaborative research.

#### Abstracts
##### **Tracing the Scientific Discovery Capacity of LLMs to Their Training Data**
2611.03617v1 by Arda Uzunoglu, Mahler Revsine, Michael Schatz, Benjamin Van Durme, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Daniel Khashabi, Eric Nalisnick

Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise for scientific discovery, yet basic questions remain about what enables reliable discovery. How can we trace the scientific discovery capacity of LLMs to their training data? We study this question as one of missing probability mass and develop a theoretical account in which monofacts-the facts observed once during training-set the discovery capacity. In materials science and regulatory genomics, we directly manipulate monofact rates in training sets and find that more monofacts consistently yield more discoveries across two open-weight model families, sampling strategies, and reasoning budgets. Further experiments show the relationship cannot be explained by training-set diversity alone and can transfer across scientific domains when paired with in-domain data. Together, our results identify monofact rate in training data as a key factor governing discoveries in language models.

摘要：

##### **Can AI Do Formal Demography? Why Vaccinating the Oldest Saves Both the Most Lives and the Most Years of Life**
2611.03500v1 by Joshua R. Goldstein, Kenneth W. Wachter

Can AI contribute to formal demography? We report an experiment in which an AI model, Claude, was asked to explain and then simplify the proof of an earlier result: that vaccinating the oldest against COVID-19 saves both the most lives and the most years of life, because the years saved per person treated rise with age. The model proposed a new proof that is shorter, more intuitive, and somewhat more general than the original, built from a known life-table identity and the definition of convexity. It states the condition for the result as the convexity of the reciprocal of the hazard, which can be checked from the hazard alone and holds for most familiar parametric models, and it yields a lower bound on life expectancy whenever that condition is met. Prompted by a further question, the model also gave an exact form of the condition, showing how far convexity can be relaxed. The model traced these identities and related results in earlier literature. An edited record of the sessions is provided, and complete transcripts are available on request. The exercise suggests that AI can revisit established results, make them easier to understand, and prove valuable generalizations.

摘要：

##### **Mixed-Methods Exploration of Sociotechnical Factors to Improve Adoption of Crime-Reporting Apps in Urban Communities**
2611.03376v1 by Olalekan David-Banjo, Chinazunwa Uwaoma, Paul Witman

Crime-reporting mobile applications can enhance community safety and engagement in urban areas, yet their adoption remains low, partly because developers prioritize technological features over residents' social realities. This study used an exploratory sequential mixed-methods design to identify and validate the sociotechnical factors that drive adoption. Two focus groups and an analysis of 660 app reviews revealed that users' initial sense of usefulness fades, whereas sustained engagement depends on perceived value (PV), comprising six dimensions: safety, anonymity, recency, autonomy, camaraderie, and accessibility. A survey of 505 urban residents, analyzed with PLS-SEM, validated PV as a formative higher-order construct formed by four of these dimensions (safety, anonymity, autonomy, and camaraderie). PV was the strongest predictor of behavioral intention, ahead of effort expectancy, facilitating conditions, and performance expectancy, and behavioral intention strongly predicted actual use. The model explained 75% of the variance in behavioral intention and 51% in actual use. The findings offer design principles for community-centered crime-reporting technologies.

摘要：

##### **The SIMPLE Law for Energy Cost of LLM Inference**
2611.03330v1 by Hiari Pizzini Cavagna, Andrea Proia, Daniele Cesarini, Zeynep Kiziltan, Andrea Bartolini

Generative AI is billed per token, yet tokens are not equally expensive. Each token enters the KV-cache and raises the cost of those that follow, so energy grows non-linearly along a sequence whose tokens look identical. No analytical model states what a token costs, and the most accurate SOTA model is a fit, not a law: it absorbs architecture into fitted coefficients, requiring per-LLM fitting. We derive closed-form expressions of the computational and memory-access complexity of LLM inference in terms of architectural hyperparameters, covering MHA and GQA, and compare a family of analytical models of increasing granularity. From these, we obtain the SIMPLE Inference Model to Predict LLMs Energy (SIMPLE), a four-parameter law that separates prefill from decode, and decode into KV-cache cost and next-token computation. Over traces of 13 dense LLMs from six families on NVIDIA H100 GPUs, it attains R2 = 0.969 and 11.97% MAPE, 3.1-3.5x below the SOTA, without per-LLM calibration. The SIMPLE law explains as well as predicts: prefill energy grows quadratically with the hidden dimension, so larger LLMs suffer most from long inputs; and more aggressive GQA KV grouping reduces the per-token energy by up to 3x relative to standard MHA. Increasing the reasoning token budget pushes output length beyond the analytically derived energy minimum, driving super-linear energy growth; although accuracy grows with the budget, energy grows faster, so Intelligence per Joule peaks at an intermediate budget and falls by up to 3.14x across a 4.0x reasoning-budget range. Agentic serving raises energy per request by 13.0x relative to non-agentic ones, driven by the growth of both the prefill and the KV-cache terms with input length; while prefix caching removes most of the prefill cost (-93%), the KV-cache term is unaffected, so even a cached agentic request costs 4.8x more.

##### **From FAIR to AI-FAIR Metadata-First Infrastructure for AI-Enabled Science**

Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies and their capabilities have advanced rapidly over the past few years, leading to widespread interest and adoption in the biological and ecological sciences. However, the data infrastructure and computational resources that provide the foundation for AI workflows in ecology and biodiversity science remain fragmented. Moreover, the flow of value remains largely unidirectional: data move from collectors, through repositories and aggregators into models, and finally to research products or management actions. The derived value, such as corrected taxonomies, annotations, quality flags, and insights, rarely returns to source communities. We propose an extension of the original FAIR principles to address the needs of AI-ready infrastructure: the AI-FAIR metadata criteria, which place AI-enabled science in the context of a bidirectional Question-Data-Model triangle. This framework is designed to facilitate large-scale content-based analysis across diverse collections and ensure that the scientists generating the data benefit from the AI-enabled advances built on their data. Alongside these criteria, we introduce an agentic evaluation workflow that both rates existing datasets on the AI-FAIR and traditional FAIR criteria and provides actionable feedback where critical metadata is missing or incomplete. This modular dataset evaluation tool, paired with a domain-appropriate checklist, translates these principles into a reusable assessment of machine-learning readiness, AI-readiness for specified tasks, traceability, and ethical and CARE-aligned stewardship. Through this approach we aim to create a community-informed system to close the model data loop, so that AI also enriches data to further enable scientific discovery.

##### **A Fine-Grained Hierarchy and Benchmark for State Tracking in Language Models**
2611.03094v1 by Jackson Petty, William Merrill, Ashish Sabharwal

Theoretical limitations of transformers on state tracking---the ability to track information about entities or variables over long contexts---have motivated new recurrent language modeling (LM) architectures such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN). These architectures have, in particular, been benchmarked against the $S_5$ word problem, a theoretically hard state-tracking task that captures abilities such as code evaluation under arbitrary variable assignment. It, however, remains open whether other, simpler forms of state tracking might both be more relevant in practice and already explain observed differences between these architectures. We propose evaluating state tracking using a hierarchy of word problems with four increasing levels of difficulty: r-trivial, corresponding to immutable assignment to variables in programming; aperiodic, where assignments are mutable; soluble, where assignments are self-referential; and general finite state tracking. Theoretically, we show that the class of problems transformers are thought to length-generalize on does not even contain all aperiodic state tracking. On the other hand, we prove that GDN with positive eigenvalues expresses all aperiodic problems, and the regular languages recognized by certain transformers are precisely all soluble ones, thus providing a fine-grained picture of state tracking capabilities of various architectures. Taking these ideas to practice, we construct StateBench, a 4-tier long-horizon state-tracking benchmark. Our systematic direct training experiments across architectures largely align with theoretical expectation, albeit with interesting observations at very large scale length-generalization. Additionally, we also evaluate pretrained models---both open hybrid and closed frontier ones---on StateBench, finding that none of them robustly solves even soluble state tracking, suggesting a direction for future improvement.

##### **Spectral Degeneration of Self-Attention under Isotropic Score Geometry**
2611.02778v1 by Mengda Li, Jianfeng Yao

We study the spectral structure of the self-attention matrix in Transformer models through an idealized spectral model for the pre-softmax score matrix. Motivated by the rotational isotropy of query and key representations, our model replaces the random token-space orientation of the score matrix by its canonical singular-value representative with a logarithmic spike--bulk separation. Empirical diagnostics on pretrained vision and language transformers motivate this model: token-space singular matrices have nearly uniform eigen-angle distributions, and score spectra are dominated by a small number of effective directions. We then prove that the row-wise softmax map transforms this structure into a degenerate attention matrix: dominant modes are preserved, whereas the bulk spectral distributions concentrate at zero. Stable rank remains bounded for bidirectional attention but grows logarithmically for causal attention. Within the canonical model, these results explain how attention can preserve a small number of dominant directions while compressing the remaining bulk modes.

##### **When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis**
2611.02511v1 by Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.

##### **Machine Learning Beyond Statistical Consistency for Hybrid AI**
2611.01914v1 by Jinsook Kim, Jinho Kang

We propose a new definition of machine learning, according to which a machine learns a function when it computes the function successfully, that is, when the value it returns for each input has a proper derivation in a formal system. We extend Gödel's Representability Theorem, which equates computation with derivation, to empirical targets in two ways: the theorem is extended to the computable reals, and the data are admitted into the system as empirical definitions that record what has been observed. We prove that a machine learns a true probability if and only if two conditions are met. The Truth Condition is that its estimate converges to the true probability, which is statistical consistency. The Assurance Condition is that the machine properly derives its own convergence inside the system. Learning thus lies beyond statistical consistency, for it requires a derivation of the convergence from the record. A global assumption about the data, such as the i.i.d. assumption, does not supply such a derivation, because it leads only to a circular one, as we show for PAC learning. On the other hand, the two conditions are met for the probability of a sentence over a finite ideal corpus. A real machine can approximate a true probability only if an ideal machine can learn it precisely, that is, only in a domain that meets the two conditions. Modern AI works in such a domain, and we offer this as a clue to its success. Modern AI is hybrid as well, in two senses: First, it performs two kinds of tasks, namely probabilistic ones such as predicting the next word and deductive ones such as proving and coding. Second, it combines two parts of different kinds, a statistical proposer and a deductive verifier, for proving and coding. Our framework explains both senses of hybridity in one formal system.

The former primarily corresponds to "Aesthetic Perception": computationally identifying and representing aesthetic attributes and predicting human aesthetic evaluations; the latter primarily to "Aesthetic Understanding": computationally describing, testing, and explaining how human aesthetic perception and judgment are formed. GCA extends this core to include "Aesthetic Generation," namely artistic generation guided by computational aesthetic models. Some computational aesthetic tasks can also use non-AI methods. Research in this scope using AI algorithms and models we define as AI aesthetics, which may involve the special core and the general extension. "Aesthetic Generation" intersects with AIGC, AI art, and computational creativity, but belongs to the general extension defined here only when computational aesthetic models substantively constrain, guide, or optimize artwork generation. ##### **Meta-memorization and memorization scaling laws in transformers**

Web API recommenders return a set of APIs for a mashup request but do not check it against provider documentation, the main public evidence of an API's capabilities. The set can therefore contain unsupported or redundant APIs and miss requested capabilities. Filling every apparent gap does not fix this, because a gap may already be served, may not need its own API, or may have no documented provider. We study post-recommendation refinement, which revises a frozen recommender's output using only the request, the catalog, and provider documentation. An LLM removes unsupported or subsumed APIs by judging candidates jointly, then audits the remaining set against a requirement checklist derived from the request. For each uncovered requirement, a fine-tuned catalog-constrained Resolver proposes a catalog API or abstains, and an independent documentation check admits only supported proposals. On ProgrammableWeb with 7 heterogeneous upstream recommenders, WAR-CRAFT raises mean SetF1 from 0.558 to 0.644, above the strongest of 5 refinement baselines (62.5%), and roughly halves functional redundancy (0.127 to 0.065) without changing mean functional coverage. Gains range from +2.2 points on generative recommenders to +18.2 on fixed-cutoff rankers. The share of such pairs in the re-timed data, which we call the exceed share, tracks where the retrained policy collapses. The rule that follows has a single parameter: merge greedily while the sum stays realisable. Every command is then realisable by construction, the demonstrated geometry is preserved, and the speed-up lands only where the demonstrator left slack. ##### **Integrating Heterogeneous Echocardiography AI Outputs in Language Space: Learnable Late Fusion with a Supervised LLM**
2610.39464v1 by Ryo Takizawa, Tempei Kabayama, Chiemi Yamazaki, Shun Kitamura, Ryo Matsuoka, Yuta Ando, Satoshi Kodera, Norihiko Takeda

View- and target-specific echocardiography AI models capture useful but complementary information and may not achieve sufficient predictive performance in isolation. A learned ensemble is a natural way to combine these outputs, but heterogeneous models typically require each output to be converted into a binary decision, probability, confidence score, or fixed-length feature vector. We developed a supervised large language model (LLM)-based late-fusion module that directly aggregates view-specific predicted finding statements and predicted measurements in language space and learns a case-level fusion function. In patient-level three-fold cross-validation of 859 examinations from 646 patients, the proposed method integrated heterogeneous outputs within a single framework, performed comparably to the best-performing reference strategy evaluated, and outperformed a general-purpose LLM without task-specific training. These results suggest that a supervised LLM can combine multiple upstream models without model-specific output conversion or feature engineering that requires medical knowledge.

##### **OPERA: OPamp Design by an Expert-Taught Reasoning Agent, from Specifications to Layout**
2610.39395v1 by Vishal Saxena, Soumyajit Mandal

Analog flows from specifications to a signed-off layout rarely run without manual iteration. Recent large language model (LLM) agents size circuits with optimizer loops over the device dimensions and stop at the schematic. We present OPERA (OPamp design by an Expert-taught Reasoning Agent), in which a general-purpose LLM agent designs CMOS opamps from specifications to post-layout sign-off with open-source tools. First, the designer teaches the agent the gm/ID method: map the specifications to transconductances and minimum currents, choose gm/ID per device role from characterized lookup tables, and size from equations, leaving a few knobs to search and an explainable design. Second, the agent learns the gLayout framework and taught layout practice, and writes a reusable layout generator that closes the layout and extraction loop at designer-approved gates. In SKY130, the agent designed a shared constant-gm bias and six opamps (5T, folded-cascode and telescopic OTAs; Miller, folded-cascode and telescopic two-stage) for fun >= 50 MHz, PM >= 60 degrees, SR 25 V/μs into 1 pF and a minimum dc gain of 30 to 60 dB. All six layouts are DRC-clean and LVS-matched, and their extracted netlists meet every target at all 15 PVT corners ( fun >= 53.7 MHz, PM >= 62.1 degrees, SR >= 27.9 V/μs). The benchmark netlists are released at https://github.com/AMPICLab/AMPICBench.

##### **Proving and Improving the Platonic Representation Hypothesis with JEPAs**
2610.39302v1 by Randall Balestriero, Yann LeCun

Whether models trained on different modalities converge to the same representation of the world is central to AI. The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) conjectures that they do as they scale, but without a proof it offers neither a guarantee nor a recipe. We turn the hypothesis into a theorem. First, we prove a \emph{Platonic Representation Theorem}: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) trained separately on any number of modalities learn the same representation up to one rotation whenever their positive pairs are two observations of one scene--\emph{good views}--and their misalignment is bounded otherwise. Second, we show that next-token prediction is a JEPA with \emph{bad views}, parts of a single document, which explains why language models align with vision only through scale. Third, we validate the theory from 145k captions to the texts of 940M web images, and uncover a scaling law of alignment that predicts unseen runs within seed noise. Good views need 42 times fewer training samples than next-token prediction on the same corpus to reach the same alignment, and a 109M-parameter text JEPA aligns with DINOv2 and CLIP's image encoders better than every language model we evaluated, up to 72B parameters, and than CLIP's text encoder: next-token prediction is a remarkably inefficient route to platonic representations. As a showcase, one rotation turns separately trained encoders into an image captioner (CIDEr .838 on COCO). Even that rotation needs no pair: on the latest pair-free benchmark, our text JEPA beats every zero-shot accuracy reported with language models up to 8B parameters, e.g., 64\% against at most 56\% top-5 on ImageNet-100. Positive views, and not scale alone, are thus the lever to build platonic representations.

##### **When Should AI Decide, Defer, and Deviate? Responsible Automation for Deceased Donor Kidney Allocation**
2610.39045v1 by Madeleine Pollack, Thomas Daillak, Mehmet Toner, Korkut Uygun, Heidi Yeh, Swati Gupta

We ask when an AI trained on historical decisions should be allowed to recommend decisions, defer to human experts, or in other cases, flag concerning patterns in data and deviate from past practice. This can be challenging because past decisions record what was done rather than what should have been done, and where data is sparse, a model's predictions may be too uncertain to act on. We provide a framework for addressing this problem using a case study in deceased-donor kidney allocation. Nearly 30% of kidneys recovered from deceased donors in the United States are never transplanted, largely because they are hard to place through the standard waitlist sequence. One can use AI to flag kidneys at risk of delayed or failed placement for out-of-sequence (OOS) allocation, but this comes at the cost of bypassing candidates higher on the waitlist. Thus, OOS allocation should only be used when the organ is demonstrably at high risk of discard on the standard sequence. Using Organ Procurement and Transplantation Network match-run data from 2016-2019 and 2023-2025 (excluding COVID-19-disrupted years), we build the Arbitrator framework, which combines various interpretable models and acts only when the uncertainty in their predictions cannot change the preferred action. Our Arbitrator resolves 93.5% of organ placement decisions using explicit medical criteria learned from data, and defers the remaining 6.5% to a human or a more complex model. We show that the Arbitrator correctly identifies 70.1% of historically discarded low-KDPI kidneys as OOS candidates, projecting that up to 43.9% of historically discarded kidneys could be flagged for expedited placement and potentially transplanted. We then exploit the interpretability of the Arbitrator to create an agentic audit comparing the learned rules to published clinical literature in order to flag decision-making patterns that are contentious.

摘要：

##### **From Grind to Grasp**
2610.38728v1 by Peter Sunehag

Discussions of complexity, knowledge and intelligence often overlook the work involved. For example, outputs generated by short programs have low algorithmic complexity but can have high computational depth, the least work needed to produce them from near-shortest programs. Producing them with less work can require longer descriptions, giving high work-bounded complexity. We introduce a formalism that compares achievements under work and information limits and describes how learning and persistence can make deep achievements shallow by reusing products of earlier work. For an agent with a limited work budget, the value of building and maintaining such resources depends on how they support its goals over later tasks. We define grasp as performance across work budgets at a fixed information allowance. Low grasp can still yield success through grind at high cost in work, without necessarily improving human grasp. Applied to improvement itself, grasp gives a criterion for recursive self-improvement in which successors make comparable further improvements more efficiently. Another route to faster progress is to expand the resources brought to bear through knowledge sharing and coordination across society, even while comparable advances require increasing work. Grasp also applies to collectives, which may outperform their members yet require more total work than one system with their combined resources. Establishing and sustaining organisation can itself be deep even when the primary task is simple. These demands may help explain the richness of biological and social systems. In agent collectives, this organisational work can generate goals beyond assigned tasks, including maintaining the collective itself. We therefore caution against allowing an emergent agent society to assume authority over social coordination, since it could increasingly direct work towards its own persistence and goals.

##### **Beyond fabrication: Misattributed numbers in papers written by AI research agents**
2610.38691v2 by Mosab Alfaqeeh, Omar Mohammed Horani, Mou'th Hourani, Hashem Alfaqeeh

Verifying numerical claims in research written by AI agents is an important but challenging problem. Recent benchmarks show that many agent-written papers fail integrity checks, but such verdicts, often produced by a single LLM judge, identify failure without explaining its nature, and existing checks either test a paper for internal consistency or test whether a reported value appears somewhere in an agent's output. We re-analyze all 540 released judgments from a recent public benchmark of AI research agents (nine agents, fifteen open research problems) and manually label 256 numerical claims from 18 submissions. Failures concentrate on alignment between the paper and the underlying work rather than overt cheating, and about four in five unsupported claims correspond to values in the agent's own files that come from the wrong experiment, metric, or run. We introduce a deterministic claim-to-evidence checker that scores whether the surrounding context identifies the claimed result and calibrates each claim against numerical decoys. With its rules fixed before labeling, it certifies claims with over 90% precision against blinded annotators (78 of 85; Cohen's kappa = 0.89), while verifying only a subset of claims; most claims it cannot verify are nevertheless traceable, so failure to verify is not evidence of falsehood. An LLM verifier given the same evidence recovers more traceable claims but accepts about three times as many misattributed ones, so the two are best used together. Applied to 99 submissions, the checker reveals large differences in traceability across agents, and on 30 submissions a second judge configuration, with a different model and harness, orders the agents' vendor groups as the official judge does while passing twice as many papers. These findings motivate provenance-first auditing of AI-produced research.

##### **Governance-Aware Federated Learning for Multi-Agent AI Systems: A Policy-Driven Framework for Trustworthy Collaborative AI**
2610.38411v1 by Kiranmayee Janardhan, Karthick S, Madhusmita Panda, Bharathi Vokkaliga Ganesh, Syed Ahmed

Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative model training without exchanging raw data, but conventional FL provides limited runtime governance, evidence verification, fairness assessment, explainability monitoring, and auditability. This paper proposes Governance-Aware Federated Learning (GAFL), which combines independent governance-evidence verification, temporal trust, fairness and explanation assessment, anomaly detection, policy enforcement, normalized aggregation, and tamper-evident audit logging. Client-reported governance values are authenticated, bound to their submitted model updates, and independently recomputed before aggregation. A cross-silo prototype with 10 participants was evaluated on the Adult Income dataset under honest, benign non-IID, biased-data, malicious-update, non-compliant, falsified-evidence, and mixed-threat scenarios. Four methods were compared across five random seeds: FedAvg, FedAvg with anomaly filtering, GAFL without independent verification, and complete GAFL. Complete GAFL intervened on all affected participants at the final round across the evaluated risk scenarios, including identifying 100% of falsified-evidence participants, whereas GAFL without verification identified none of the falsified-evidence participants. Final macro-F1 ranged from 0.7520 to 0.7733 across GAFL scenarios. Complete GAFL required 173.67 plus or minus 26.94 ms of governance processing per round, compared with 3.82 plus or minus 0.79 ms for FedAvg. ##### **A Tutorial on Generative Models for Music Audio**
2610.38252v1 by Mateusz Modrzejewski, Joanna Kurkowska, Dorien Herremans

Music audio generation spans instrumental clips, full songs with vocals, edits of existing recordings, and systems that play alongside a musician in real time. This monograph explains the methods behind these systems for readers familiar with neural-network training who are new to music generation. It introduces neural audio codecs and autoencoders, autoregressive and masked token models, diffusion and flow matching, sampling acceleration, and preference optimization in a shared notation. Worked examples connect training objectives and sampling procedures to published systems. The application sections relate the musical requirements of instrumental generation, songs, editing, and live performance to model design. Comparison tables record representations, methods, inputs, tasks, and release information documented in papers and official sources. Symbolic composition, speech, and sound effects are included where they connect to music audio. We explain how to interpret automatic metrics and learned judges, design and report listening tests, and assess evidence about data provenance, memorization, attribution, and output detection. Selected legal decisions and disputes illustrate the implications for training and release. The reviewed systems combine established generative paradigms with new representations, planning stages, and post-training procedures. Differences in data, scale, and evaluation limit what cross-system comparisons can establish about the sources of reported gains.

##### **Precedent or Intent? What Coding Agents Follow When Evolving Existing Code**
2610.38244v1 by Haichao Zhu, Geyang Xu, Zhaorui Yang, Jiyuan Wang, Qian Zhang

Existing code reveals past implementation decisions but rarely explains the reasoning behind them. When coding agents implement new features or modify existing functionality, past decisions may conflict with new task instructions. This raises a fundamental question for agentic software evolution: when multiple implementations satisfy the same functional requirements, how do coding agents decide whether to follow existing code or new task instructions? We investigate this question through bidirectional, controlled experiments in purpose-built repositories, followed by validation in real-world open-source repositories. Our approach isolates three competing sources of guidance: (1) agents' default preferences, (2) repository precedents, and (3) explicit task instructions. We conduct 5,625 trials across four coding agents and six implementation pairs, with validation in four open-source repositories spanning three programming languages. We find that agents share the same default implementation choices, yet overwhelmingly follow repository precedent when it conflicts with those defaults (359/360 trials). Crucially, not all precedents carry equal influence: three supporting local files yield 96% adoption, compared with only 4% for sibling-resource files, and changing file placement alone can shift adoption from 0% to 100% at a fixed example count. When new task intent conflicts with existing code, written priority rules achieve substantially higher success than code examples alone (59/60 versus 38/60 joint successes for two agents on two implementation pairs). The effectiveness of explicit rules also extends to open-source repositories, where a harness-loaded rule raises joint success from 0/120 to 117/120. These findings motivate design-decision traceability that preserves not only implementation choices but also the rationale and priorities that should guide future changes.

##### **Disparity Laundering: Consensus Without Fairness in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.38215v1 by Xiaoyang Wang, Tianrui Wang, Christopher C. Yang

Clinical large language models are increasingly fielded as multi-agent teams, on the assumption that deliberation averages out individual error. It does not average out bias. Across a full factorial of five model families and four aggregation topologies on 120 real MIMIC-IV emergency cases under counterfactual demographic swaps, no configuration neutralizes the disparity: all 20 cells commit the direction of their agents' independent answers, giving Black patients more acute triage. Every model has a topology that amplifies its baseline gap, but which one varies by model, so no design is reliably safe or reliably amplifying: the disparity is relocated, never removed. Late-stage fixes fail: fairness instructions and demographic masking leave the gap untouched, and per-agent self-debiasing widens it, because the bias is already encoded in the agents' ordinary clinical numbers. We call this disparity laundering; the audits now applied to clinical AI, per model, per output, per version, can certify the parts but never the committed decision. Interventions close the gap exactly when they make counterfactual versions visible inside the system and pool them, through deliberation, into the committed decision. A diverse-perspective ensemble does both while keeping the team, with no retraining or architectural change: the disparity falls by 67 to 94% where all five perspectives pool at every round, and the one topology that never pools them is the one it cannot reliably repair. Medical multi-agent systems do not filter demographic bias; they launder it into plausible decisions no single-version audit can catch, and the only defense is to make the bias visible.

##### **The Prestige: Benchmarking Cognitive Visual Reasoning using Magic Tricks**
2610.38103v1 by Shuo Wen, Beixi Du, Edwin Meriaux, Junming Shi, Chloe Si, David Meger, Doina Precup, Gregory Dudek

We introduce The Prestige, a video understanding benchmark that probes cognitive visual reasoning through professional magic performances, where deliberate misdirection challenges models to track events and explain unexpected outcomes. The benchmark comprises open-ended questions ranging from temporal and causal understanding to robustness against adversarial questions. ##### **Are Seismic Foundation Models More Than Glorified Feature Extractors?**
2610.38003v1 by Laura Laurenti, David Liu, Brian Kulis, Men-Andrin Meier, Christopher Johnson

Earthquake sequences are stochastic on two separated timescales: the seismic waves of any individual earthquake propagate over seconds to minutes, while the earthquakes themselves arrive as a sparse, self-exciting occurrence process, with inter-event intervals from seconds to months. Respecting both scales in one training objective is the open challenge. Foundation models (FMs) for seismic waveforms instead import their learning targets from audio and vision, where every target is computed from a single fixed-length window. Such a window holds one realization of the wave propagation (an earthquake trace), never the occurrence process that decides when earthquakes happen. We develop nine model designs sharing one Mamba encoder-decoder, trained on a fixed corpus of 3M windows, varying the self-supervised objective, quantization bottleneck and mixer; the two key comparisons are architecture-matched, varying the objective alone. Each checkpoint is evaluated frozen on three downstream tasks. Varying the target redistributes downstream skill rather than raising it, and no checkpoint wins everywhere. Adding masking pressure to our strongest classifier re-specializes it rather than making it a generalist: it gains 6.9 points of few-shot P-wave picking precision and loses 9.6 points of few-shot classification accuracy. By the standard imported from language and vision, these models are feature extractors rather than FMs. Controls on capacity, architecture, and domain proximity do not explain why skill moves rather than rises. Against task-specific baselines the advantage is regime-dependent: our checkpoints beat them few-shot on classification and ground-motion prediction, and trail them on full data. We argue the binding constraint is the target, and that settling the question requires a learning target defined across events, the timescale that makes seismicity stochastic in the first place.

##### **MuonGS: Pretraining LLMs with Relaxed Weight-Norm Control**
2610.37647v1 by Athanasios Glentis, Dawei Li, Mingyi Hong

Weight decay and explicit weight-norm constraints improve optimizer performance in language model pretraining, yet a unified understanding of why such control helps and how much is needed remains incomplete. We analyze norm control in Muon over the entire training trajectory using the \emph{accumulated effective learning rate}, $C_T=\sum_{t<T}η_t/\|W_t\|_F$, where $η_t$ is the nominal learning rate and $η_t/\|W_t\|_F$ is the effective learning rate relative to the weight norm. For bounded update directions, accumulation over any training interval bounds relative weight displacement. We show that vanilla Muon can accumulate only logarithmically, potentially limiting exploration and helping explain its slower empirical progress, whereas MuonW and MuonH guarantee at least linear accumulation under the stated conditions. Going beyond bounded-norm control, we show that sufficiently slow norm growth guarantees faster accumulation than the logarithmic worst case, opening up less restrictive optimizer designs.
  Based on this insight, we propose MuonGS, which uses Gram--Schmidt (GS) projection to remove the radial component of each Muon update without fixing the weight norm. With bounded update directions and a constant nominal learning rate, we prove that the weight norm grows at most proportionally to $\sqrt{t}$ after $t$ steps. Consequently, MuonGS guarantees $C_T=Ω(\sqrt{T})$, with accumulation sustained even in later training. Further, for smooth scale-invariant objectives we establish its $O(T^{-1/2})$ stationarity guarantee. ##### **Why and How to Do Science When AI Is Better at It: A Framework Grounded in Human Understanding and Direction**
2610.36116v2 by Hoda Heidari, Aydin Mohseni

As AI begins to match or exceed human researchers at core scientific tasks, two questions become pressing: \emph{why} should humans keep doing science, and \emph{how} should they use AI when they do? This paper argues that the second can be answered only by answering the first. It holds that one indispensable aim of science is \emph{durable, collective human understanding of the world, pursued in service of human ends and values}. From this principle it derives a two-part test for when a research task can be delegated to AI. The first part, \emph{human understanding}, requires that the scientific community remain able to explain, defend and contest the resulting claims, and retain the skills to do so. The second, \emph{human direction}, requires that judgments about which questions matter and what findings mean stay with accountable humans. Applying the test to each phase of research in the Contributor Roles Taxonomy (CRediT) yields recommendations that fields can tailor to their questions and methods. The framework differs from existing guidance in three ways. It rests on an account of what science is for rather than on integrity norms. It holds even if AI outperforms human researchers, rather than depending on AI's current limitations. And it judges each use of AI by its effects on human understanding and direction rather than by the type of tool, across every phase of research, including peer review and the training of new researchers. It closes with the reforms needed to keep science aimed at human understanding and direction.

##### **Center-of-Mass Interpretation of Evolution Strategies**
2610.35865v1 by Akshat Gupta

Evolution strategies (ES) update a model by sampling a population of perturbed copies, scoring each one, and moving in the reward-weighted direction of the perturbations. This update is usually presented as an estimate of a smoothed gradient. We present a geometrical interpretation of this update instead. Treat each perturbed model as a particle and its reward as the particle's mass. Before scoring, every particle has the same mass and the population balances at its centroid. Once rewards are assigned, it balances at its reward-weighted center of mass instead. We show that the mean-subtracted ES update is exactly this displacement, scaled by a scalar factor that depends on the mean reward, for any finite population. The physical reading needs nonnegative rewards, which can always be arranged by adding a constant to every reward without changing the update. In the limit of a large population or under antithetic sampling, the centroid sits on the model itself, so ES has an even simpler geometric interpretation: from its current location, the model moves towards the reward-weighted center of mass of its own population. The same picture explains what subtracting the mean reward does: it changes the point from which the displacement is measured, from the model to the sampled centroid. Our contribution is an intuitive, geometric way to visualize an ES update, exact for any finite population.

2610.35753v1 by Fan Xu, Hao Wu, Zhi Song, Hanbo Huang, Wenzhuo Ma, Nan Wang, Wei Gong, Xibin Zhao

Multivariate time series produced by industrial installations and server clusters are under continuous monitoring, and anomaly detection is the first line of defense before a local fault escalates into a system-wide failure. In the absence of labels, the dominant paradigm trains a reconstruction model on normal data and scores the residual to reach a verdict. Recent work has steadily enriched the expressiveness of the recovery map through Transformers, diffusion models, and cross-scale associations. A residual, however, only certifies that an observation can be recovered; it does not certify that the variables still explain one another as they do under normal operation. A coordinated yet unfamiliar operating transition inflates the residual without breaking any relationship, whereas a sensor drifting away from its peers may leave the residual silent. Methods that fold inter-variable dependencies into the reconstruction objective inherit this ambiguity, because their relational evidence is ultimately supervised by the same recovery loss. We therefore propose CLOSURE, which evaluates recoverability and cross-channel consistency as two normal-data prediction tasks learned in separation. The recoverability task is solved by a diffusion reconstructor conditioned on a discrete memory of normal temporal bases. The consistency task is solved by a relation-closure operator that predicts every channel exclusively from the others, where the target's own trajectory is withheld and sparse signed gates decide which peers are actually used. The two scores are reconciled only through calibration on normal data. Extensive experiments show that CLOSURE consistently outperforms recent reconstruction-, frequency-, and topology-based detectors on public benchmark streams, confirming the value of examining consistency alongside recoverability.
