Next, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) supplies documents, while chain-of-thought prompting encourages intermediate steps and agents use tools. FlashAttention lowers training and running costs by reducing data movement during attention; quantized low-rank adaptation (QLoRA) saves fine-tuning memory. Readers get a life cycle figure and tables comparing methods and their limits. A short checklist for judging LLM evaluation claims flags test material in training (data contamination) and model-based grading (LLM-as-a-judge). It also covers very long inputs (long context) and hallucination, meaning false or unsupported claims. Check factual accuracy separately from whether an answer sounds helpful.

摘要：

##### **In-Context Adapter Generation for Compositional Generalization in Large Audio Language Models**
2610.34413v1 by Yiwei Guo, Siqi Huang, Bohan Li, Jing Peng, Hankun Wang, Zhihan Li, Kai Yu

Large audio language models (LALMs) have achieved strong performance on standard speech and audio understanding tasks, yet their ability to systematically generalize to novel task compositions remains underexplored. Taking a meta-learning view, we extend Text-to-LoRA (T2L) to LALMs, generating instruction-conditioned LoRA adapters with a Transformer hypernetwork rather than learning a single fixed adapter shared across instructions. To exploit fine-grained task information, we introduce in-context adapter generation (ICAG), a token-level hypernetwork architecture; we call the resulting method T2L-ICAG. We evaluate this approach on two representative LALMs using SIFT-50M for supervised fine-tuning and construct a controlled compositional evaluation suite covering five task classes. Across two LALM backbones, T2L-ICAG consistently improves over static LoRA and also the global embedding-based T2L baseline. Coverage and capacity analyses further show that these gains are larger on structurally out-of-domain tasks and cannot be explained by parameter count alone. These results suggest that generating task-specific adapters from instructions is a promising direction for improving compositional generalization of LALMs.

摘要：

##### **Fed-Universe: A Semantic-Geometric-Topological-Human (S-G-T-H) Stack for Negotiated Alignment in Federated Systems**
2610.34009v1 by Ting Xu, Henry Leung

Conventional federated learners implicitly optimize for global means, creating a "one-size-fits-all" paradigm that inevitably suppresses the minority under cross-site heterogeneity. To bridge this critical gap, we present Fed-Universe, a generalizable Semantic-Geometric-Topological-Human (S-G-T-H) architecture that transforms passive averaging into an active negotiation for decentralized alignment. The S-layer deploys an edge-capable LLM as a semantic surrogate to translate heterogeneous inputs into standardized patient profile prompts. The G-layer enforces geometric quality assurance (GQA) via a mapping f: Theta -> Q from the statistical parameter space to a physically interpretable quality domain, employing a cosine similarity gate to retain minority updates based on physical geometry rather than statistical proximity. The T-layer executes topological Pareto control, identifying a sensitivity-based knee point (lambda_k) to balance multi-objective aggregation without majority degradation. Finally, the H-layer operationalizes a cognitive twin dynamic to mirror the user's real-time psychological state, dynamically adapting human-computer interaction modes via active intent alignment. To ground this theoretical architecture empirically, we validated the S-G-T core on a binational synthetic clinical network simulation. This proof-of-concept demonstrated a "zero-sum escape": a minority node (<3% data volume) achieved utility parity (loss reduction from 0.857 to 0.340) alongside dominant hubs. Our Fed-Universe framework is being expanded along two focused pillars: institutional multimodal alignment (Fed-Ultra) and individual cognitive intent alignment (Fed-Human).

摘要：

##### **Network Topology Does Not Matter: The Breakdown of Classical Consensus in LLM Populations**
2610.37699v1 by Sahil Rajesh Dhayalkar

As multi-agent language model networks become widely deployed, predicting their collective behavior is a critical challenge. Classical distributed consensus theory relies on network topology to predict convergence, structurally assuming that agent updates are convex combinations, population means are conserved, and weights are content-independent. In this work, we demonstrate that these foundational assumptions fail for large language models (LLMs). By deterministically extracting continuous credence from token-level probabilities across eight instruction-tuned models and 73,080 controlled exchanges, we directly measure the empirical LLM update operator. We find that LLMs systematically violate convexity by updating outside the hull of observed opinions, exhibiting active repulsion (negative weights) that fundamentally breaks row-stochastic modeling and standard bounded-confidence repairs. Scaling these empirically fitted operators to simulations of 10,000 agents reveals that algebraic connectivity explains almost none of the final outcome variance. Instead, population dynamics are governed entirely by the model's internal update bias and message framing. Consequently, populations of several models, including those with up to 7B parameters, reliably compound small local drifts to reach confident agreement on objectively false claims regardless of their network structure. Our results prove that classical graph-theoretic metrics are inadequate for LLM populations, highlighting a profound deployment risk where multi-agent deliberation predictably saturates in collective delusion.

摘要：

##### **Extraction meets Distillation - cryptanalytic techniques to recover parameters from a Deep Neural Network**
2610.33671v1 by Biprarshi Biswas, Arya Anshuman Ojha, Yubaraj Das, Devansh Khosla, Kamran Rais, Saankhya Samanta, Subhamoy Maitra, Pantelimon Stanica

We study fully connected neural-network reconstruction by combining cryptanalytic weight-direction extraction with learning from predicted labels. We adapt a ReLU extraction implementation to Leaky ReLU, compare sign-search methods, and estimate biases and output layers. We prove conditional bounds for direction recovery with approximate prefixes, preservation of local distance votes, prediction changes from aligned parameter errors, and sign selection with independent scoring data. We also specify exact row restoration and distinguish its guarantee from that of gradient masking. The experiments evaluate assisted extraction, with privileged access to gradients, hidden activations, and victim parameters, followed by label-based reconstruction. Across eight search seeds on the largest synthetic architecture, Leaky ReLU recovers 51--57 neurons in the deepest hidden layer, while ReLU recovers none. This advantage does not hold uniformly across architectures and layers. Removing victim-weight access from the sign walk gives less accurate signs, despite high final prediction agreement. Across 40 seeded runs on the two smallest synthetic victims, agreement on 5,000 inputs excluded from fitting and selection ranges from $0.9890$ to $0.9992$. On two CIFAR-10 victims, the reconstructed models achieve $56.40\%$ and $58.29\%$ agreement and exceed the distillation baseline on three functional metrics. We report label requests separately from privileged access and explain why prediction agreement does not identify network parameters.

摘要：

##### **Jointly Modeling Preferences and Imperfect Decision-Making in Large Language Models**
2610.33651v1 by Axel Abels, Elias Fernández Domingos, Tom Lenaerts

Do preferences Large Language Models (LLMs) express in standard elicitation tasks predict their strategic behaviour? LLM agents interacting with humans and other AI systems face potentially conflicting objectives, such as individual welfare, group welfare, and environmental concerns. Prior work characterizes LLM preferences through behavioral-economic and psychological elicitation or strategic games, but rarely tests consistency across settings and prediction in other strategic environments. This requires modeling how competing objectives jointly shape behavior, whereas existing approaches typically measure preference dimensions in isolation. Drawing on utility theory, we introduce the Preference-Behavior Model (PBM), which combines a preference representation with mechanisms capturing imperfect utility maximization. We formulate estimation as an inverse-game problem, jointly inferring preferences and behavioral parameters from observed choices. We evaluate PBM in three social dilemmas that expose conflicting motives: a Public Goods Game, a Collective-Risk Dilemma, and a Common-Pool Resource dilemma. We find that while preferences estimated from established behavioral economic elicitation instruments do not consistently predict behavior in these dilemmas, models fitted in one dilemma retain predictive value in others, suggesting that inferred preferences capture meaningful aspects of LLM decision-making that elicitation instruments may miss. Finally, we assess alignment with persona instructions, finding that inferred preferences broadly reflect intended priorities with specific divergences across dimensions, LLMs, and games being identified by our approach. Our methodology provides a framework for studying how consistently LLM preferences explain behavior, assessing alignment with intended priorities, and revealing trade-offs and behavioral patterns that standard elicitation may miss.

摘要：

##### **When Generated Information Returns: Error Persistence in LLM-Mediated Information Ecosystems**
2610.33617v1 by Yi Liu

Large language models (LLMs) increasingly produce online articles from material that later becomes input to other models. This circulation connects individual responses to the information available for subsequent generation. We study a consequential distinction: an article can avoid endorsing a false claim while preserving the concrete details that enable its reproduction. Our framework connects claim content, expressed stance, and downstream reuse to explain selective error persistence under a continuing supply of accurate and inaccurate information. We implement a controlled community writing process that varies whether published articles return as inputs, matching external supply, input capacity, and generation volume. Across twelve factual targets and four production models, recirculation increases the presence of complete false claims by 7.29 percentage points at the final round while reducing explicit endorsement by 8.33 points. Effects differ across facts and models. Paired interventions then abstract eligible error details within fixed histories while preserving correct content and the surrounding controversy. Error reproduction remains largely unchanged across the evaluation targets, while a development quotation target exhibits local reductions. These findings connect persistent errors to the information available throughout a writing process and motivate evaluation of both what models affirm and what their publications supply to future writers.

摘要：

##### **Auditing evaluation artifacts and data leakage in large language model systems for rare disease diagnosis**
2610.33592v1 by Jian Yang, Peilin Liu, Xiaohan Li, Songda Li, Yu Cao, Yuqing Feng, Haomin Li

Augmented large language model (LLM) systems have reported high benchmark accuracies in rare disease diagnosis, yet their real-world clinical utility remains unproven. A recent meta-analysis flagged augmented LLM diagnostic evaluations as having a high risk of bias due to suspected data leakage; however, the precise impact of these vulnerabilities on model inference has yet to be fully quantified. In this study, we present a comprehensive empirical audit to quantify evaluation artifacts across prevailing AI paradigms, using a repository of over 60,000 cases from diverse public and independent clinical cohorts. By reconstructing a state-of-the-art agentic framework, we demonstrate that candidate-space contamination artificially inflates performance: enforcing strict cross-dataset isolation resulted in a marked performance decline, with the cross-dataset mean Recall@1 decreasing from 54.3% (under conventional one-vs-rest evaluation) to 40.3% (Hedges' g = 0.94). Similar spatial overfitting in supervised classifiers caused diagnostic accuracy to drop from an internal mean of 42.9% to 16.7% across external cohorts (Cohen's d = 2.01). Furthermore, we quantify how LLMs infer diagnoses by exploiting retrospective therapeutic cues in clinical narratives, and how temporal misalignment in foundational knowledge bases leads to performance fluctuations. We conclude that prevailing evaluation protocols for augmented LLM systems inadvertently reflect data leakage rather than genuine medical reasoning. To address these systematic artifacts, we propose a rigorous evaluation framework - coupling strict data-isolation protocols with standardized benchmarking safeguards - to ensure future diagnostic AI for rare diseases is evaluated on actual clinical robustness.

摘要：

##### **Energy Efficient Lung Tumor Segmentation with a modified CBAM ENet Skip Model**
2610.33479v1 by Sharan Thummagunti, Aayushi Padia, Abhiram Koppuravuri, Prabhu Prasad B M, G N Girish

Lung tumor segmentation plays a vital role in early detection and treatment planning of lung cancer. Although state-of-the-art deep learning models achieve high dice score coefficients (DSC), each forward pass requires computing large amounts of floating point operations, making them computationally expensive, requiring GPU acceleration, and having slow inference speed, limiting real-world clinical deployment. We propose a lightweight, energy-efficient model that leverages the ENet architecture for its real-time inference capability and low computational cost, enhanced with UNet-style skip connections between encoder and decoder stages for feature reuse and the lightweight Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM) for improved feature representation. The model is trained on publicly available data from The Cancer Imaging Archive (TCIA) via the Medical Segmentation Decathlon Challenge (MSD) and evaluated on the Lung Image Database Consortium and Image Database Resource Initiative (LIDC-IDRI) dataset. Our model achieves a DSC of 0.90812 (threshold of 0.5) with its compact architecture of 430,052 parameters and 0.549 Giga Multiply-Accumulate operations (GMAC), making it up to 10 times faster, requires 79 times less GMAC than UNet, and has 22 times fewer parameters than SegNet. These characteristics make it well-suited for deployment in clinical settings where computational resources are limited and decrease the cost of utilizing such a system. Additionally, the explainable AI technique of Grad-CAM is used to provide visual interpretability of the model's predictions and to identify regions where segmentation performance requires improvement.

摘要：

##### **Data Optimization for LLMs: A Survey**
2610.33405v1 by Ou Wu

Large language models (LLMs) have attracted great attention in both academic and industrial communities in recent years. Numerous survey studies have covered various aspects of LLMs, such as learning strategies, applications, alignment, explainability, and evaluation. However, little headway has been made in reviewing data-related aspects for LLMs, despite data being recognized as a key factor contributing to the emergence of the LLMs era. A wide range of data optimization algorithms and techniques are employed in LLMs' training and application processes. These techniques often originate from diverse deep learning domains, with their theoretical inspirations and heuristic motivations appearing unrelated to each other. This study aims to develop a comprehensive framework for LLMs' data optimization techniques to enhance data utilization and efficiency. We first discuss data challenges in LLMs' training and inference. Second, we summarize data perception dimensions for evaluating LLMs' data properties. These dimensions primarily include quality, difficulty, diversity, and redundancy, laying a foundation for subsequent optimization. Third, existing optimization techniques are reviewed and categorized into four groups: data selection, augmentation, reweighting, and others. Fourth, we analyze interconnections between these categories. Finally, we identify key challenges and future directions for LLMs' data optimization compared with conventional deep learning approaches.

摘要：

##### **TempShap: When and How Much a Feature Matters in Time-Series Forecasting**
2610.33330v1 by Chi Zhao, Anastasiia Chelnokova, Jing Liu, Elena Parilina

Attribution for a time-series forecaster should answer two questions that a feature-level scalar cannot: when in the input window a feature acts, and how much its influence changes across the forecast horizon. Most existing Shapley explainers treat each feature as one player and therefore collapse both axes. We propose TempShap, a retrospective, loss-based Shapley framework whose players are the individual input cells $(t,i)$. Its attribution tensor yields temporal-localization and horizon-impact profiles. We validate these against planted temporal structures, a tractable exact cell game, and a closed-form attribution oracle for a real linear forecaster. Permutation sharing estimates the cell-level game at localized perturbation cost. Under redundant inputs, one-at-a-time scores under-credit substitutable information, whereas the signed Shapley decomposition preserves total value and yields more reliable held-out deletion rankings under mean masking. A planted counterexample isolates the redundancy mechanism. Experiments show a deletion-ranking advantage across Shapley estimators, forecasting benchmarks, and model families. TempShap therefore makes fine-grained Shapley attribution both practical and operationally useful.

#### Abstracts
##### **DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control**
2611.03601v1 by Chengxi Li, Shaoxuan Li, Deyao Hong, Qingle Liu, Yi Huang, Puheng Bian, Minzhi Xu, Lanyue Zhang, Mingze Sun, Xin Li, Calvin Xiao, Shengyuan Wang, Huan-ang Gao, Jiazheng Xu, Kaisen Yang, Hongning Wang

As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.

摘要：

##### **PlantVLM-Edge: Quantized Vision-Language Models for On-Device Crop Health Diagnosis**
2611.03557v1 by Gaurab Wagle, Bishal Adhikari, Jiajia Li, Xiaolei Huang, Dong Chen

Timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is essential for sustainable agricultural production, yet accurate diagnosis often requires expert knowledge that is not readily available. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in visual understanding, reasoning, and natural-language interaction, creating new opportunities for intelligent crop health assessment. However, deploying VLMs on resource-constrained devices remains challenging due to their substantial computational and memory requirements. This paper presents PlantVLM-Edge, a quantized vision-language framework for conversational crop health diagnosis that can run entirely on resource-constrained edge devices. PlantVLM-Edge combines agricultural domain adaptation, post-training quantization, and on-device serving with an offline chat interface, allowing growers to ask open-ended questions and receive diagnoses, symptom explanations, and management guidance without cloud connectivity. The framework is backbone-agnostic: we validate it with two different VLM types with varying scale and architecture (Qwen3-VL-2B and LLaVA-1.5-7B), four quantization schemes, and two diagnostic domains (plant diseases and insect pests), and we deploy all variants on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin. Eight-bit quantization affects answer quality minimally relative to the full-precision model, and 4-bit activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ) retains 98.6% of full-precision answer quality on both domains while reducing the weight memory of the 2B backbone by 49%. In its most compact form, PlantVLM-Edge requires 2.01 GiB of weight memory, returns its first token in about 0.12 s, and completes a 64-token diagnostic response in 0.78 s. These results demonstrate that timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is practical on edge devices, providing a foundation for next-generation crop monitoring and decision support systems.

摘要：

##### **Contrastive Adapter Training and Consensus Knowledge Distillation for Multi-Source-Free Domain Adaptation in Skin Cancer Diagnosis**
2611.03528v1 by Ali Abedi, Q. M. Jonathan Wu, Ning Zhang, Farhad Pourpanah

Skin cancer diagnosis, particularly differentiating melanoma from benign nevi, is challenging due to the visual similarity between lesions. Although convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs) have shown promising performance, their effectiveness often deteriorates on data from heterogeneous clinical sources. Conventional domain adaptation methods address domain shift but require access to source data during adaptation, which is often infeasible due to privacy regulations. Multi-source-free unsupervised domain adaptation (MSFDA) addresses this limitation by leveraging multiple labeled source domains to generalize to an unlabeled target domain without requiring access to source data. However, existing MSFDA methods rely on full backbone fine-tuning, leading to catastrophic forgetting and overfitting on small clinical datasets, and address domain shift at the aggregation stage without establishing a shared domain-invariant feature space. Furthermore, their reliance on hard pseudo-labels or confidence-weighted aggregation introduces noisy supervision signals under domain shift. To address these limitations, we propose CAT-CKD, consisting of two components: (1) contrastive adapter training (CAT), which trains lightweight ConvPass adapters within a frozen ViT backbone using supervised contrastive learning (SCL) to establish a shared domain-invariant feature space before source-specific model training, and (2) consensus knowledge distillation (CKD), which aggregates logits from multiple source models into a consensus supervisory signal and adapts a student model on unlabeled target data using KL divergence. Experiments on five publicly available skin lesion datasets show that CAT-CKD achieves an average AUROC of 86.1%, outperforming existing MSFDA methods while requiring only 4.3M trainable parameters. The code is available at https://github.com/A-Abedi/CAT_CKD.

摘要：

##### **From FAIR to AI-FAIR Metadata-First Infrastructure for AI-Enabled Science**
2611.03327v1 by Elizabeth G. Campolongo, Eric R. Sokol, Marty Downs, Gina Glenne, Gregory E. Maurer, Sydne Record, Dustin R. Rubenstein, Matthew J. Thompson, Christine Laney, Hilmar Lapp, Max Tarjan, Tanya Berger-Wolf

Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies and their capabilities have advanced rapidly over the past few years, leading to widespread interest and adoption in the biological and ecological sciences. However, the data infrastructure and computational resources that provide the foundation for AI workflows in ecology and biodiversity science remain fragmented. Moreover, the flow of value remains largely unidirectional: data move from collectors, through repositories and aggregators into models, and finally to research products or management actions. The derived value, such as corrected taxonomies, annotations, quality flags, and insights, rarely returns to source communities. We propose an extension of the original FAIR principles to address the needs of AI-ready infrastructure: the AI-FAIR metadata criteria, which place AI-enabled science in the context of a bidirectional Question-Data-Model triangle. This framework is designed to facilitate large-scale content-based analysis across diverse collections and ensure that the scientists generating the data benefit from the AI-enabled advances built on their data. Alongside these criteria, we introduce an agentic evaluation workflow that both rates existing datasets on the AI-FAIR and traditional FAIR criteria and provides actionable feedback where critical metadata is missing or incomplete. This modular dataset evaluation tool, paired with a domain-appropriate checklist, translates these principles into a reusable assessment of machine-learning readiness, AI-readiness for specified tasks, traceability, and ethical and CARE-aligned stewardship. Through this approach we aim to create a community-informed system to close the model data loop, so that AI also enriches data to further enable scientific discovery.

摘要：

##### **Multimodal Prompt Learning with Dual-Granularity Contrastive Alignment for Preoperative Prediction of Lung Adenocarcinoma Invasiveness Using 3D CT**
2611.03109v1 by Qing Huang, Xiaotong Tan, Lintong Yao, Wufeng Xue, Ruobing Huang, Dong Ni, Hao Wu, Tian Xiao, Jun Cheng

Accurate preoperative prediction of lung adenocarcinoma invasiveness is important for surgical planning and individualized treatment. However, overlapping CT appearances make it challenging to distinguish invasive adenocarcinoma (IAD) from Pre-IAD lesions. We propose a multimodal prompt-learning framework with dual-granularity image-text alignment for 3D CT-based IAD classification. A 3D ResNet-CBAM encoder extracts global and spatial visual features. A frozen RadBERT backbone is adapted with separate learnable prompts for report-level and keyword-level text encoding. These prompts are initialized using semantic representations derived from real-world clinical reports. Global contrastive alignment connects whole-volume CT representations with reports generated from clinician-annotated radiological attributes to provide overall semantic supervision. A multiple instance learning-based local alignment module associates spatial CT features with vascular-focused radiological keywords, to capture local imaging characteristics potentially associated with tumor invasion. The framework jointly optimizes classification and alignment objectives, while inference requires only CT images. Experiments included 1,593 pathologically confirmed nodules. On the test set, the proposed method achieved an accuracy of 87.92%, F1-score of 87.78%, and AUC of 0.9499. Its AUC significantly exceeded that of the image-only Res-CBAM baseline with the same visual backbone (0.9297; DeLong test, p = 0.0078). Ablation analyses supported the complementary contributions of global and local alignment and the benefit of report-based prompt initialization. Qualitative visualizations showed activation within nodules and adjacent vascular structures. These findings suggest that dual-granularity radiological supervision can enhance CT-based invasiveness prediction while preserving a CT-only inference workflow.

摘要：

##### **CAREFUL-Bench: Effect-Aware, Judge-Free Evaluation of Multi-Turn LLM Function Calling**
2611.02978v1 by Alexander Kovalenko, Artem Redchych

The multi-turn checker of the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard (BFCL) evaluates final state correctness but ignores the effects of intermediate calls: extra calls are free, function documentation does not describe side effects, and the scripted user never responds to proposals. Consequently, models asking for confirmation before destructive actions are penalized; 260 of 734 reference turns in the base category perform destructive actions without confirmation. We make BFCL effect-aware without replacing it. We infer effect declarations for all 128 functions by executing them in BFCL simulators and comparing state changes, with destructive writes identified by three annotators. A judge-free replay scorer reproduces official results across all 800 reference trajectories while reporting unrequested destructive calls, duplicate writes, redundant reads, surplus steps, and their token costs. We also introduce a confirmation-aware protocol with a deterministic simulated user. Across 11 open models, effect-aware scoring preserves the official ranking but reveals substantial differences in execution quality: the leading model loses 14.0 points due to unrequested destructive calls, while surplus steps consume up to 28% of input tokens. Adding effect hints to function documentation has no impact on eight evaluated models. Allowing the simulated user to respond recovers up to four points for models that seek confirmation. However, the best model completes only six of 576 conversations requiring destructive actions with explicit confirmation. These results expose a key limitation of current function-calling benchmarks: they cannot distinguish between asking for clarification and obtaining permission before potentially destructive actions.

摘要：

##### **Tug of Watts: A study on the Energy - Accuracy Trade-off of Large Language Models on Medical Question Answering Tasks**
2611.02889v1 by Viju Sudhi, Giulio Billi, Andreea Scrob, Regina Stodden, Philipp Cimiano

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in medical question answering (QA) systems. While recent works aim at incrementally improving the downstream task accuracy, an empirical estimation of the models' energy consumption and the associated carbon footprints is often overlooked. In this study, we demonstrate how practitioners can estimate energy consumption and arrive at reliable emission estimates for medical QA tasks available in the OpenMedical-LLM leaderboard by investigating 13 LLMs from 3 model families ranging from 270M to 14B parameters. We discuss how one can critically interpret energy versus accuracy trade-offs for a set of models for a given task. With this work, we underline that reporting disjoint energy and accuracy metrics alone does not suffice for careful trade-off assessments, but it also demands systematic construction and interpretation of joint trade-off metrics. We envisage such studies aiding practitioners in adopting better and more efficient models for their task and helping them become active participants in building sustainable LLM pipelines.

摘要：

##### **Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks vs. Multi-Layer Perceptrons: A Rigorous Statistical Benchmark on Clinical Tabular Data**
2611.02856v1 by Rafał Wodziński, Paweł Szczerbiak

Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) have recently been proposed as a promising alternative to Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs). However, empirical evaluations on structured medical datasets remain scarce, and commonly reported metrics like AUROC can yield overly optimistic performance estimates on imbalanced clinical cohorts. In this study, we present a standardized benchmark comparing eleven architectures - nine KAN variants (FastKAN, WavKAN, ReLUKAN, ChebyKAN, TaylorKAN, GramKAN, JacobiKAN, LegendreKAN, and TabKAN), a tuned StandardMLP, and a Random Forest baseline - across seven heterogeneous medical tabular datasets spanning oncology, cardiology, nephrology, neurology, endocrinology, and obstetrics. Our experimental protocol employs a 3x5 Repeated Stratified K-Fold cross-validation scheme with nested Bayesian hyperparameter optimization and evaluates discrimination via Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC), calibration through the Brier Score, and computational efficiency. Formal equivalence testing is conducted using the Bayesian Correlated t-Test with a Region of Practical Equivalence (ROPE) at +/- 0.01. The results indicate that on datasets with severe class imbalance, dedicated KAN architectures (particularly TabKAN and ReLUKAN) can improve minority-class detection and achieve higher AUPRC than standard MLPs. Nevertheless, across pooled datasets, KAN variants demonstrate comparable overall predictive discrimination rather than a systematic advantage, while requiring 1.20x to 1.48x longer inference latency per sample and up to 12.5x more trainable parameters. Consequently, StandardMLP remains a strong baseline on the efficiency Pareto frontier, while KAN variants provide a viable alternative when precision on rare positive cases is the primary clinical objective. The code for reproducing the results is available at https://github.com/rafalwodzinski/kan_mlp_comparison.

摘要：

##### **CARE: Grounded Recursive Self-Improvement for Production Customer-Service Agents**
2611.02839v1 by Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Rena Gao, Xiaochen Zhao, Yuchen Ge, Haohang Liu, Runze Nie, Qitian Chen, Qi An, Haolin Song, Yufei Sun, Yihao Ding, Jey Han Lau, Krista A. Ehinger, Xingjun Ma, Weijia Chen, Ruiyuan Wu, Zhen Luo, Ke Xing, Yongjun Yin

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in production agents involves transforming accumulated experience into reliable model updates while adapting to evolving business policies. We present CARE (Customer-service Agent Retrospective and Evolve), a grounded RSI framework that operationalizes this process in customer service. Its goal is to replace a frontier proprietary incumbent with an in-house mid-sized model that continues learning after deployment. After a one-time cold start on governed production traces, the current model serves production traffic alongside the incumbent, and its own failures drive the next update. A 24/7 inspection process establishes failures through evidence-blinded, independently reviewed judgments. Attribution separates model errors from defects in Skills, tools, or the harness, routing component failures to repair. Model-attributable trajectories are truncated at the first erroneous decision and paired with verified repairs. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) then updates the model using the same model as teacher, with the diagnosis and repair supplied as privileged information unavailable to the student. To support continued evolution as business requirements change, training mixes failures from different Skill/SOP versions, each judged against the policy in force when it occurred. Candidates run an executable simulation test compiled from the latest SOP and then frozen, followed by offline inspection of the resulting traces. Promotion requires a combined simulation-and-inspection score at least at parity with the deployed version, confirmation through an online comparison with the incumbent, and human approval.

摘要：

##### **Where Is a Prediction Model Miscalibrated, and for Whom? Local Audits in Outcome-Learned Pockets**
2611.02639v1 by Bernardo Modenesi, Taoyi Chen, Sima Najafzadehkhoei, Lucia Wang, Ameya Diwan, Beatrice Knudsen

Overall and group-level averages, and ranking metrics such as the AUC, can hide sets of observations for which a prediction model systematically over- or under-predicts. We present an audit that asks where a model is miscalibrated (in which sets of similar observations it over- or under-predicts on average) and for whom (whether its average prediction error differs between demographic groups, such as by race or sex, within the same set). It groups observations into pockets using random-forest similarity, which weighs each feature by its importance for predicting the outcome, with a ridge-regression grouping alongside. Pockets are learned on one sample and tested on another, with simultaneous confidence intervals that allow for correlation within units such as hospitals; the audit needs only features, outcomes and fixed predictions. In 17 simulation scenarios with an error planted for one group, the combined audit located it better than the strongest alternative grouping in 15, with false-alarm rates near or below 5%; with a local error planted for everyone, it won in 12 of 15 scenarios at the smaller error size and lost when the error was unrelated to the outcome. In public synthetic data from the care-management algorithm studied by Obermeyer et al., it found no detectable overall error but under-prediction of illness in the sickest pockets and over-prediction elsewhere, and distinguished Black patients' concentration where the score under-predicted for everyone from a Black-White gap within pockets. For APACHE IVa, an exploratory analysis located its previously reported over-prediction of ICU mortality in a mostly ventilated pocket and a low-risk pocket, with none detected in a moderate-risk one. Two chest X-ray classifiers showed offsetting local errors behind no detectable overall error. Code: https://github.com/modenesi/pocket-audit

##### **Measuring AI Agents as Teammates with Collaborative Intelligence**
2611.02633v1 by Sean Wu, Aneesh Pappu, Jiaru Zou, Yubin Kim, Yuncong Mao, Hongjian Zhou, Junchi Yu, Jieneng Chen, James Zou

Progress in AI is often measured by what a single model can do on its own. Yet teams of AI agents are starting to make breakthroughs and discoveries in science, medicine, mathematics and software engineering. As AI begins to work in teams of agents and people, the ability to collaborate becomes a core part of their intelligence. Research on human teams has measured this ability for decades. Inspired by this, we introduce the Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark, which measures how well an AI agent works as a teammate. It contains 240 tasks across 8 dimensions of teamwork, from refusing an unsafe shortcut to checking the work that a weaker model leaves behind. Each task comes from a real record or an existing benchmark, from aviation accident reports and clinical cases to coding tasks and negotiations, and has a programmatically verifiable outcome. To isolate teamwork from individual capability, models are scored only on tasks that they can solve by themselves when they have full information. We evaluate 18 closed- and open-weight models and find that every model, across sizes and families, has clear weaknesses as a teammate, with the best model scoring only 82.2 out of 100. Frontier models almost always refuse unsafe shortcuts and report misconduct, yet they cave under interpersonal pressure, reaching the best overall deal in only 14% of conflicts, and their pass rate drops from 97% to 81% when they take over a task in which a weaker model has cheated. Crucially, general capability does not guarantee good teamwork, as models with nearly the same capability score differ by up to 11 points as teammates. The benchmark includes 160 public tasks with code and data, along with 80 held-out tasks for evaluation. As AI agents increasingly work alongside other agents and people, the ability to collaborate effectively is becoming a crucial capability to measure and develop.

摘要：

##### **When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis**
2611.02511v1 by Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.

摘要：

##### **Benchmark of THReD: Evaluating an LLM Agent and Its Preprocessing Tools for Turning Electronic Health Records into AI-Ready Datasets**
2611.02484v1 by Javad Mohammad Alizadeh, Huanmei Wu

LLM agents are increasingly built into data-analysis software, yet little evidence shows how reliably they preprocess clinical data or whether the tools they invoke give correct, reproducible output. Medical artificial intelligence agents should retrieve patient identifiers only when a task requires them, the data-minimization principle behind the HIPAA minimum necessary standard for protected health information (PHI). Stronger boundaries reduced this access, and the residual access under the strictest policy was confined to frames in which the user overrode the policy or asserted approval. 2611.01313v1 by Zain Naboulsi

Post-trained language models fabricate citations at high rates. We ask whether that is reward hacking: scoring well on a proxy for scholarship while doing worse on what it stands for. The clearest result holds the weights fixed and changes only the prompt. Asking for citations raises fabrication sharply in post-trained checkpoints and not in base ones: a 47.8-point interaction on twelve checkpoints (Holm-adjusted p = 2e-34) and 41.5 points on fifteen, in all three domains. A length-matched control separates the citation cue from a demand for a longer answer, so the effect is specific to citations, not verbosity. Post-training also raises fabrication overall, from 4.8% to 56.7%, but our registered prediction that it would rise most where fabrication is most tempting did not hold. A preference model trained only on surface citation features prefers post-trained output 86% of the time, and holding citation count fixed shows it rewards quantity. Our answer: possibly, but not proven. Post-trained models produce fabricated citations at far higher rates when asked for sources, and a naive evaluator that counts sources without checking them would prefer their output for citing more. But the targeted pattern we registered did not appear, and plain instruction-following could explain the same results. Proving that the reward causes fabrication needs an experiment that changes the reward during training, which this study does not do. Pre-registration: doi:10.17605/OSF.IO/S53HY. ##### **Capacity Planning for Resource-Constrained Clinical LLM Serving**
2611.01152v1 by Yi Tang, Geoffrey Martin, Yiye Zhang, Yifan Peng

Deploying large language models (LLMs) under a fixed accelerator budget requires jointly deciding how computational capacity is allocated across model sizes and how incoming requests are routed across that capacity. Although prior work has extensively studied LLM routing and serving efficiency, the relative importance of fleet composition and routing under resource constraints remains less understood. We investigate this question using a controlled four-GPU serving environment with heterogeneous clinical NLP workloads spanning short factual verification, biomedical semantic matching, and long-context clinical summarization. We systematically vary fleet composition, routing policy, and offered load while holding the workload trace and hardware budget fixed. Across 24 configurations, descriptive main-effect shares attribute more variation to fleet composition than to the evaluated routing policies in time to first token, latency, and long-context coverage. All-small fleets provide the best responsiveness and throughput but substantially lower long-context coverage, whereas larger-model capacity improves coverage at the cost of serving efficiency. Heterogeneous fleets provide intermediate operating points but can introduce strong-model bottlenecks when large-model capacity is scarce. In a focused evaluation of this bottleneck, capacity-aware routing reduces mean latency and increases throughput while incurring a small reduction in long-context coverage. Within this measured regime, the results support prioritizing capacity allocation, with routing managing demand within the provisioned capacity.

Bias in transformer-based models is a growing concern as these architectures become more prevalent in automated decision-making systems, such as prediction models for hiring, lending, and clinical treatment recommendation. Mechanistic interpretability (MI) is a growing body of work that measures meaningful causal effects of activations on model output by identifying human-interpretable features encoded in model activations. When such features correspond to sensitive attributes, they provide a direct way to quantify the bias those attributes introduce and to causally intervene to mitigate it. However, counterfactual fairness (CF) methods have not been systematically adapted to this setting of intervening on interpretable mechanisms in transformers. We propose a framework, Mechanistic Interpretability for Counterfactual Fairness (MICF), that utilizes MI tools to reformulate CF constraints in terms of transformer activations, unifying former approaches into a single pipeline for fairness analysis in transformers. ##### **Confident Fools or Careful Searchers? A Behavioural Study of When LLMs Search Beyond Their Static Internal Memory**
2610.41124v1 by Nipun D. Pathirage, Kwan Srijomkwan

Agentic LLM assistants must repeatedly decide whether to answer from memory or to call a tool such as web search. Unnecessary searches cost latency and money; missing ones yield confident but unsupported answers. Prior work mostly measures verbalised confidence or trains new adaptive-retrieval methods, and rarely audits the search decisions that deployed products actually make. We introduce a benchmark of 400 multiple-choice questions that combines widely known inside-knowledge (IK) questions, which should be answered directly, with outside-knowledge (OK) questions about facts from news published after the models' training cut-offs (to our knowledge), which should trigger search or, without tools, abstention. Each item offers an abstain option, and two action-level scores, NAS and KIS, reward searching on OK questions and restraint on IK questions. Across five commercial LLMs with and without their vendors' web search, GPT-5 mini and Grok 4 Fast search on over 90% of OK but only 1% of IK questions, whereas Mistral Small searches on just 20% of OK questions, often abstaining instead of using its tool; one product's search indicator proved unusable. Searching is partly triggered by explicit temporal cues: on uncued OK questions, the NAS of the two selective models falls from about 0.72 to 0.57--0.58. The scores thus measure the search triggering of specific products, not metacognition in a strict sense.

##### **BABA-Bench: Evaluating Long-Horizon Patient-Advocacy Agents**
2610.41076v1 by Shriram Vasudevan, Videet Mehta, Connor Sweeney

An LLM agent that helps a patient get care must do two things. It must reach the correct outcome, and it must follow the necessary procedure. For example, the agent must get the patient's permission before it shares the patient's records. We introduce BABA-Bench, a benchmark of 47 interactive worlds. We built each world from a de-identified, real patient advocacy case that expert advocates reviewed. In each world, the agent must find hidden information and coordinate insurers, clinics, suppliers, pharmacies, and community organizations. The engine checks each action at the time the agent does it. It checks if the agent has permission, if the agent has evidence for the action, and if the action is prohibited. We evaluated 14 frontier models with three runs on each world, for a total of 1,974 runs. Agents often reached the correct outcome by an invalid route. Of the 921 runs that reached all required outcomes, 254 (28%) did not follow the necessary procedure. Claude Opus 5 reached all required outcomes in 81.6% of its runs, but only 52.5% of its runs passed all checks. When we gave two models a verified route, their pass rates increased from 29.8% to 91.5% and from 44.0% to 97.2%. For these models, the main problem is to find a route, not to execute it.

##### **Towards Understanding Score-Seeking in LLMs**
2610.41014v1 by Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Alex Mallen

Production large language models sometimes reward-hack, exploiting flaws in how tasks are scored rather than performing the tasks as intended, sometimes in highly sophisticated and adversarial ways. We investigate the motivations driving this behavior in GPT 5.6 Sol, GPT 5.5, and a checkpoint of OpenAI o3 without safety training ("exp-rl-cap-late") by observing model behavior in agentic environments where the grader is leaked. In our environments, the models appear to be score-seeking: trying to score well according to graders that they believe assess their current actions, even at the expense of violating explicit user instructions. They appear to care about score itself rather than its downstream consequences, violating instructions at approximately the same rate whether the score is indicated to be used for training, for evaluation, or not at all. We observe evidence that the more recent models care to some extent about scores that do not assess their own current actions. GPT 5.6 Sol and GPT 5.5 help other agents 24.6 percentage points more often on average when the other agent stands to gain a score of 1 rather than 0 from being helped. ##### **The Athlete as a Scientist of One: Agentic Bayesian Experimental Design for Individualized Physiology from Consumer Wearables**
2610.40370v1 by Behnam Moradi

Large language model (LLM) agents for personal health read wearable data and recommend, but do not learn about the individual in a way that can be audited or falsified. Claims about the athlete live in an evidence-gated hypothesis registry: each must issue a probabilistic prediction about a future session before it occurs, and its belief moves only through computed evidence from a logged statistical fit. Predictions come from a mechanistic digital twin inferred from heart rate, GPS and barometric altitude: a lagged heart-rate response model, a fitness-fatigue model with learned time constants, and a latent technical-descending skill state, measured on discovered trail segments. The agent prescribes the next training week as a constrained experiment maximizing information gain subject to load and safety constraints, around two instrument rides. We evaluate the system two ways over eighteen months of data. A rolling-origin replay benchmark shows the twin beats standard forecasters on heart rate but not segment time, and the agentic loop matches a static model. A live prospective phase runs the weekly cycle: in its first three weeks the ledger sealed 20 predictions, scored 14, the descent forecast calibrated after one correction, the climb forecast pessimistic as the athlete improved, and the safety officer vetoed the agent twice. Every correction is preserved in the registry, a template for auditable, falsifiable personalization in digital health. We release the registry, benchmark and protocol.

Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can limit its effectiveness at short, computationally affordable horizons. ##### **Learning to Reason from Reference Samples Alone: Reinforced Adversarial Learning for Modern LLM Post-Training**
2610.38851v2 by Albert Bou, Javier Valdes-Aleman, Jon M. Laurent, Samuel G. Rodriques, Andrew D. White, Michaela Hinks, Siddharth Narayanan

In many settings where LLM post-training is desirable, high-quality answers are recognizable but difficult to score with explicit rewards. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is limited to tasks where an explicit correctness signal is available. Prior adversarial approaches to training reasoning LLMs either complement verifiable rewards with co-trained critics or require reference reasoning traces. We ask whether a co-trained LLM judge can turn reference answers alone into the sole reward for on-policy post-training of reasoning LLMs. We call this method Reinforced Adversarial Learning (RAL). We first validate RAL on three verifiable tasks (integer multiplication, code-based arithmetic-expression synthesis, and SMILES-to-formula translation), using the verifiers only for evaluation. With sufficiently capable judges and enough training, RAL matches direct RLVR. We then apply RAL to scientific abstract generation in two settings. In the first setting, RAL guides the generator to produce abstracts in the scientific domain of the reference abstracts, even though the prompt leaves that domain unspecified. In the second setting, we generate abstracts for U.S. National Institutes of Health grant proposals conditioned on the grant title. Human scientists and an ensemble of LLM judges find our generations harder to distinguish from real abstracts than those of frontier-LLM baselines. These results suggest a path toward post-training reasoning models in scientific and other high-value domains, where increasingly capable judges infer hard-to-specify reward functions from example answers.

摘要：

##### **Latent Decision Flow Rewards for Clinical Guideline Reasoning**
2610.38709v1 by Gaurav Bhatt, Marinka Zitnik

Clinical practice guidelines encode evidence-based recommendations as conditional decision flows, which makes them a natural source of process supervision for training reasoning models. Two properties of clinical reasoning make this supervision difficult to use. A decision flow consistent with a guideline does not by itself determine the correct answer for a given patient, which we call the path--answer gap, and several valid decision flows can support the same clinical decision, which we call path multiplicity. Current training approaches address parts of this problem. Answer rewards supervise the final decision, and process rewards computed against a reference flow supervise one valid decision flow per case. Supervised fine-tuning on annotated flows teaches guideline-consistent reasoning but constrains the model to one trajectory per case and can reduce answer accuracy. We introduce GUIDER, a method that jointly optimizes a decision flow controller and a reasoning policy. The controller learns to generate plausible decision flows from guideline-derived supervision, and the policy learns to generate a decision flow and a final answer for each patient case. GUIDER computes the process reward for the policy over several controller-generated flows, which accounts for path multiplicity, and gates this reward on answer correctness, which addresses the path--answer gap. Joint training allows the process supervision to evolve with the policy. On MedGUIDE, GUIDER with Qwen3-14B achieves 82.5 weighted accuracy and 73.5 guideline adherence, 3.3 and 3.1 points above the strongest clinical reinforcement learning baseline. With Qwen3-14B and Gemma4-12B backbones, GUIDER also improves over CRPO, MRPO, and FaithMed on the out-of-domain benchmarks AMEGA-LLM, RealMedQA, and CancerGUIDE.

##### **Benchmarking decision models against generative large language models on medical multiple-choice questions**
2610.38448v1 by Oscar Freyer, Daniel Glauert, Moritz Hofmann, Max Ostermann, Stephen Gilbert, Jakob Nikolas Kather

Medical workflows often require selecting among predefined options. We compared 13 decision models with 15 generative large language model configurations, including different reasoning settings, using 1,273 four-option MedQA questions under text-only conditions. Accuracy ranged from 67.71% to 89.08% for decision models and from 78.32% to 96.86% for generative configurations. Among the tested configurations, Mercury Decide had the lowest mean final-attempt cost (USD 0.0069 per 1,000 questions), while Clef Omni had the shortest median request duration (0.291 seconds). Decision-model probabilities distinguished correct from incorrect answers, but error rates among answers assigned probabilities of at least 0.90 ranged from 1.71% to 12.77%. Answer agreement across three runs ranged from 92.30% to 100%, while probability repeatability varied. Decision models offered resource advantages alongside lower peak accuracy; selecting models for medical workflows requires considering probability quality and repeatability together with accuracy and the resources needed to detect and correct errors.

##### **DES-JEPA: Learning a Shared Vocabulary for High-Dimensional Discrete Event Sequences**
2610.38445v1 by Hugo Math, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Christos Anagnostopoulos

Temporal foundation models such as Chronos and TimesFM generalize across domains through a shared numerical observation space. In contrast, discrete event sequences (DESs), including operational event logs or patient clinical-event trajectories, have domain-specific event vocabularies whose labels lack a consistent correspondence across domains. Beyond this lack of consistency, the number of distinct events in real-world settings is high enough to challenge both classical temporal point process modeling and current in-context foundation inference models such as FIM-PP. Critically, current foundation models for DESs are limited to a small, fixed number of event types, restricting their applicability to realistic, high-dimensional event streams. To address this, we propose two complementary modules: (i) DES-JEPA, a joint-embedding predictive architecture that learns a shared discrete vocabulary from synthetic stochastic processes with unaligned event vocabularies; and (ii) DES-PFN, a discrete event sequence prior-fitted network that reuses the learned vocabulary for in-context next-event prediction. Without task-specific fine-tuning, this framework achieves competitive zero-shot performance on eight unseen real-world datasets and scales up to 700 distinct event types.

##### **LLMs as Judges and Examinees for Medical Item Difficulty Prediction**
2610.38402v1 by Xiaomeng Xiong, Marcus Walker, Yanlin Jiang

Predicting medical item difficulty from item text alone remains challenging. Prior work has largely relied on linguistic features or text representations derived from general-purpose and domain-specific language models, but predictive performance has remained modest. This study takes a different approach by using difficulty-related signals that emerge when open-weight large language models (LLMs) interact with test items. Using USMLE and PANCE medical licensure examination items, we examined LLMs in two roles. As judges, the models provided absolute difficulty ratings and pairwise difficulty comparisons. As simulated examinees, they generated response-based signals including correctness, keyed-option probability, response entropy, and the probability margin between the top two options. We then combined the strongest judgment and response signals with baseline item features in downstream prediction models. Pairwise difficulty judgment was the most consistent individual LLM signal across both datasets, while simulated-examinee signals and absolute judgments provided complementary information in some feature combinations. The best configurations reached a Spearman correlation of $ρ$ = .447 on USMLE, exceeding the best system in the 2024 BEA shared task ($ρ$ = .25), and $ρ$ = .494 on PANCE. These results were comparable to direct absolute judgments from the proprietary frontier model Claude Opus 5 ($ρ$ = .443 and .488, respectively). Overall, judgment and response signals from open-weight LLMs captured difficulty-related information beyond text representations. Because these models can be deployed locally, the approach offers a practical option for predicting the difficulty of unreleased or high-security test items.

##### **SparseTokenGS: Gaussian-based Sparse-View CBCT Reconstruction via Reusable Token Pool**
2610.38360v1 by Zihan Deng, Zhenzhen Gong, Yujie Liu, Jiaxin Gong, Dingkun Liu, Kexian Xie, Yixing Huang, Peng Jin, Tianye Niu, Zhisheng Wang, Fenglin Liu, Hongming Shan, Lequan Yu

Sparse-view cone-beam computed tomography is widely used for fast, low-dose volumetric imaging and lesion localization in dentistry and image-guided interventions, but it introduces severe artifacts. Feed-forward Gaussian methods transfer the reconstruction cost to training and decode each coordinate from projection-aligned features and neighboring anchors. However, information from the whole scan is written only when the field is constructed and cannot be retrieved during coordinate decoding, leaving the volume inconsistent within the scan. This is a common limitation of such methods: once the field is fixed, decoding uses only local features and reconstruction quality drops substantially. We propose SparseTokenGS, a Gaussian-based method for sparse-view cone-beam computed tomography reconstruction via a reusable token pool. Anchor Token Fusion and Point-Token Attention query this shared pool at the anchors and at the query coordinates, respectively. The local Gaussian prior is retained, and the volume is reconstructed in one forward pass, without per-scan optimization or a projection-domain loss. On three public chest and dental datasets, SparseTokenGS achieves the highest reconstruction performance among the compared methods. On clinical dental scans, reconstruction from only six projections reaches 29.51 decibels in peak signal-to-noise ratio and a structural similarity of 0.842 against the full-view reference, and off-the-shelf tooth-and-pulp segmentation keeps these structures separable for root-canal localization. Experiments on volcanic rock and chicken show performance after same-object adaptation. A reusable token pool gives feed-forward reconstruction a mechanism that keeps scan-wide context available through decoding and can benefit future rapid clinical lesion localization and analysis. The code is available at: https://github.com/FrankDengAI/SparseTokenGS.

##### **Rubricon: Automated Rubric Generation and Refinement for LLM Evaluation and Training**
2610.38220v1 by Ulyana Tkachenko, Hui Wen Goh, Guram Gogia, Jonas Mueller

Rubrics enable reward assignment on tasks without verifiable answers, but are expensive to manually produce from scratch. We present Rubricon, a pipeline that generates discriminative rubrics without requiring human preference labels, rubric annotation, or generator-model training. Using any LLM, Rubricon samples multiple rubrics, selects the most discriminative candidate, adds criteria to capture overlooked requirements or response flaws, and prunes criteria that are misaligned or shallow. Alongside LLM self-critique and test-time scaling, Rubricon uses observed criterion pass rates on example model responses to guide refinement. Across five professional, consumer, clinical, and general-use LLM benchmarks, Rubricon produces rubrics whose scores of frontier LLM responses exhibit 9% greater alignment with labor-intensive expert-authored rubrics, compared to the best alternative synthetic rubric generation method among RRD, QSRG, OpenRubrics, and agentic generation via harnesses like Codex. On HealthBench, post-training a Qwen3.5-9B model with Rubricon rubrics yields 4.5% better performance than training with rubrics from the strongest alternative synthetic rubric generator, RRD.

##### **Disparity Laundering: Consensus Without Fairness in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.38215v1 by Xiaoyang Wang, Tianrui Wang, Christopher C. Yang

Clinical large language models are increasingly fielded as multi-agent teams, on the assumption that deliberation averages out individual error. It does not average out bias. Across a full factorial of five model families and four aggregation topologies on 120 real MIMIC-IV emergency cases under counterfactual demographic swaps, no configuration neutralizes the disparity: all 20 cells commit the direction of their agents' independent answers, giving Black patients more acute triage. Every model has a topology that amplifies its baseline gap, but which one varies by model, so no design is reliably safe or reliably amplifying: the disparity is relocated, never removed. Late-stage fixes fail: fairness instructions and demographic masking leave the gap untouched, and per-agent self-debiasing widens it, because the bias is already encoded in the agents' ordinary clinical numbers. We call this disparity laundering; the audits now applied to clinical AI, per model, per output, per version, can certify the parts but never the committed decision. Interventions close the gap exactly when they make counterfactual versions visible inside the system and pool them, through deliberation, into the committed decision. A diverse-perspective ensemble does both while keeping the team, with no retraining or architectural change: the disparity falls by 67 to 94% where all five perspectives pool at every round, and the one topology that never pools them is the one it cannot reliably repair. Medical multi-agent systems do not filter demographic bias; they launder it into plausible decisions no single-version audit can catch, and the only defense is to make the bias visible.

##### **PromptSense: Sentiment-Aware Mental Health Chatbot with Dynamic Prompting**
2610.38044v1 by Sugi Lee, Jianlong Zhou, Jing sUN

Current Large Language Models (LLMs)-based mental health chatbots often lack sufficient empathy, contextual awareness, and safety mechanisms, potentially leading to ineffective or harmful interactions. This paper proposes a novel framework that integrates sentiment and depression analysis with a rule-based filtering mechanism prior to model inference. By dynamically constructing prompts based on emotional and risk-related insights, the framework aims to significantly improve the contextual relevance, empathy, and risk-aware of model-generated responses. We evaluated four model strategies and two prompt engineered variants across qualitative metrics such as empathy, helpfulness, clarity, relevance, safety, as well as toxicity assessments using the Perspective API. Experimental results indicate that the prompt engineering approaches outperforms DSFT and RLAIF by 1.8\% and 1.1\% respectively.

##### **Algorithmic Bias from the Perspectives of Healthcare Professionals**
2610.38006v1 by Jennifer Xu, Tamara Babaian

This paper focuses on algorithmic bias of machine learning and artificial intelligence applications in healthcare information systems. Based on the quantitative data and qualitative comments from a survey and interviews with healthcare professionals, who have different job roles (e.g., clinical vs. administrative), this study provides findings about the relationships between algorithmic bias, perceived fairness, and the intended acceptance and adoption of ML algorithms and algorithm generated outcomes. The results suggest that the opinions of healthcare professionals toward the causes of algorithmic bias, the criteria of algorithm assessment, the perceived fairness, and bias mitigation approaches may vary depending on their job roles, perspectives, tasks, and the algorithm characteristics. More research is needed to investigate algorithmic bias to ensure fairness and equality in healthcare.

##### **Responsible Quantum Machine Learning for Medical Imaging: A Fairness Audit Across Sensitive Subgroups**
2610.37819v1 by Giovanni dos Santos Franco, Felipe Mahlow, Ellison Fernando Cardoso, José Adenaldo Santos Bittencourt, Felipe Fanchini

Quantum machine learning (QML) is increasingly explored for medical classification, yet its behaviour under subgroup-aware fairness criteria remains understudied. We present an empirical fairness audit of classical and quantum classifiers for medical imaging, assuming QML is not inherently fair. Two scenarios are evaluated: dermatological lesions on DDI (sensitive attribute: Fitzpatrick skin type) and chest X-rays on CheXpert-derived data (sensitive attribute: age group). Using ResNet-18 embeddings, we train SVM, MLP, QSVM and QCNN under a baseline and three mitigation settings: differentiable Equalized Odds (EO) regularisation, Gradient Reversal Learning (GRL), and both combined. Foundation models (PanDerm, EVA-X) serve as external references. Quantum classifiers may achieve competitive aggregate performance while still exhibiting subgroup disparities. Since a smaller subgroup AUC gap may stem from degrading the best group rather than improving the worst, each mitigation is also tested against a pre-specified non-inferiority margin relative to its baseline. Under this criterion, several configurations with the smallest disparity, notably QCNN with GRL on DDI, are not clinically acceptable due to loss of global discrimination. On DDI, QSVM with GRL+EO performed best and approached PanDerm in the performance/fairness trade-off; on CheXpert, QCNN with GRL achieved the highest aggregate performance but increased disparity, and EVA-X remained stronger. Fairness effects are model- and dataset-dependent, so QML requires empirical subgroup-aware evaluation. All quantum models run in classical simulation; we claim no quantum advantage.

##### **TeamGym: A Gym for Team Decision-Making by AI Agents with Distributed Information**
2610.37571v1 by Chanwoo Park, Jaywoong Jeong, Zachary Wojtowicz, Yubin Kim, Taekyun Lee, Kaiqing Zhang, Asuman Ozdaglar

Many real-world decisions require teams to integrate information distributed across participants. Yet AI agents are still primarily trained and benchmarked in isolation. We introduce TeamGym, an environment for evaluating and training language model-based agents on team decision-making problems constructed from real-world legal, finance, medical, and software engineering corpora. TeamGym introduces a method to identify a minimal set of facts, each element of which is necessary and all of which are collectively sufficient for a correct final decision, which we call pivotal information. This enables TeamGym to both control the distribution of pivotal information and track how well agents identify and integrate pivotal information when making team decisions. Examining 8 language models on 12 source corpora, we find that current AI agents often fail to effectively share information: Distributing pivotal information across participants lowers success rate by 22.9 percentage points on average relative to giving the same information to a single model (a communication gap). We also document that participants do not effectively retrieve decision-relevant information on their own: A team's success rate is lowered by a further 19.9 points when participants must identify and extract pivotal information from source material rather than receiving it directly (a retrieval gap). To help remedy these deficits, we use TeamGym to train policies through reinforcement learning on team decision loss. Across two policy-training experiments, we find that training improves final-decision quality and induces emergent coordination in participation, speaker selection, and communication, even though the reward evaluates only the final decision.

##### **When uncertainty quantification fails: structural vulnerabilities and corrections for selective prediction under clinical class imbalance**
2610.37561v1 by Walter Endrizzi, Flavio Ragni, Raffaele Marchesi, Stefano Bovo, Monica Moroni, Giuseppe Jurman, Venet Osmani

Background. Selective prediction systems improve the safety of clinical artificial intelligence by deferring ambiguous cases to human experts while automating high-confidence decisions. However, this safety mechanism relies entirely on the premise that uncertainty scores accurately reflect clinical ambiguity. In prognostic tasks characterized by severe class imbalance, where the clinical outcome of interest is a minority, this assumption breaks down. Standard uncertainty measures cease to capture clinically aligned ambiguity; instead, they become a proxy for class membership, systematically penalizing minority patients regardless of their individual predictive clarity.
  Results. Across 56 synthetic conditions and three neurodegenerative cohorts spanning severe class imbalance to near-balanced conditions (multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's Disease, Alzheimer's Disease), our framework consistently maintained bounded, balanced risk across trajectories. In contrast, global metrics either collapsed minority Sensitivity or protected the minority at the expense of majority patients. Applying CCR with standard entropy fails to protect the minority because it breaks within-class monotonicity when tau=0.5. Combining CCR with threshold-aware metrics restores strict monotonicity, correctly identifies ambiguous patients, and safely mitigates minority exclusion.
  Conclusions. Under clinical class imbalance, standard uncertainty quantification measures dataset sparsity rather than clinical ambiguity, creating a systemic illusion of safety. This bias is a correctable structural flaw. Clinical pipelines deploying selective prediction under imbalance should adopt threshold-aligned uncertainty measures and class-stratified evaluation. This reduces class-specific risk imbalance, supporting safer selective prediction under the evaluated conditions without silently excluding the most vulnerable patients.

##### **EUReCA: End-to-End Unified CBCT Reconstruction Across Clinical Acquisitions**
2610.37740v1 by Jiening Zhu, Chengzhu Zhang, Jason Fan, LiCheng Kuo, Weixing Cai, Xiuxiu He, Laura Cervino, Jean Moran, Xiang Li, Tianfang Li, Yabo Fu

Cone-beam computed tomography (CBCT) is widely used for image-guided radiotherapy, but reducing the number of projections to lower dose and scan time makes reconstruction increasingly ill-posed. We present EUReCA (End-to-end Unified REconstruction across Clinical Acquisitions), a geometry-aware feed-forward model for variable-view clinical linac CBCT. For a given acquisition trajectory, EUReCA accepts 8-100 projections with a single set of weights, without view-count-specific retraining; the same architecture and training recipe extend unchanged to other clinical trajectories. It combines learned projection features with an in-frame Feldkamp-Davis-Kress (FDK) reconstruction. The model encodes each projection with a shared multiscale backbone, back-projects features into multiresolution 3D grids using the acquisition geometry, aggregates variable numbers of views at fixed computational cost, and refines the volume with a global 3D transformer and multilevel decoder. On simulated data, EUReCA achieved 29.9-33.2 dB PSNR with 10-100 views and outperformed all applicable feed-forward baselines; it also exceeded iterative optimization-based methods while running orders of magnitude faster. On 15 measured patient CBCT scans evaluated against vendor reconstructions, EUReCA achieved the best performance at every tested view count. These results suggest that geometry-aware, measurement-anchored feed-forward reconstruction can provide a practical and flexible foundation for variable-view linac CBCT.

2610.36513v1 by Yibo Yang, Zerui Huang, Haomiao Yang, Zixia Wang

As large language models advance rapidly, they are increasingly used to answer medical questions, and many such models are now deployed locally. Once a model is deployed, its behavior is governed mainly by the ethical policy written into its constitution. However, most existing work focuses on factual correctness rather than the trade-off between competing ethical values. Even benchmarks that do evaluate ethics treat it as a fixed property of the model rather than as a variable, so how a constitution reshapes medical-ethics behavior remains largely unexplored. Motivated by these findings, we propose a do-no-harm adaptive selector that chooses a constitution per model, matching the per-model oracle while guaranteeing a worst-case floor and generalizing to unseen models at low cost.

##### **Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation**
2610.36477v2 by Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu, Jiaru Zou, Mengting Ai, Zihao Li, Jingrui He

On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.

2610.35593v1 by Wei Zhang, Wolfgang Nejdl, Ming Tang

Ablation results further show that both sparsity regularization and the alignment component of input-space back-mapping contribute to the final explanation quality.
